Introduction 

10/6/4/7 

Little, Br

The economic system of our country is firmly rooted in the concept of ownership of property, with the ownership of real estate being rudimentary. A realization that our substantive and adjective jurisprudence has its foundation on and evolved from the common law of England, wherein real property was the determinative element of the social and political economic make-up of the feudal system, amply demonstrates the reason of the great importance of real estate. With such a back ground it was most natural that law in all its categories and classifications should be affected by or affect the subject of real property.

With the socialization of the political philosophy, with its solemn formalities, its harsh rules and antiquated customs, it became necessary to have statutory enactments and judicial interpretations to meet the growing requisites of an everchanging and modernized organization. As a premise in all evolutions, such developments have increased the complexities in the law of real estate. 

Many practicing attorneys as well as laymen active in the field of real estate, have oft times expressed their desire of a new judicial approach which would simplify the problems involved in land titles, and especially has this individual desire been aggravated upon being confronted with a defective or unmarketable title. Title insurance has not gone a long way to be fully effective and in its full efficiency there are many limitations on its successful accomplishments. The program of title registration commonly known as the Torrens System, not only involves a tremendous initial expense at this time and many difficulties as to the administration, but its adoption merely accelerates all the problems of title that are now met each time a title is analyzed. Therefore, until we have reached the stage or are willing to undertake the accomplishment in the field of law that the scientists of the new "atomic" era have developed in the field of nuclear physics, we must have the tools readily available to expedite the solution of the multitude of vexing matters frequently presented to the practicing attorney.

III 

644829

REAL PROPERTY LAW IN NEW JERSEY 

In order to give a proper opinion in a real estate transaction, the lawyer must be thoroughly familiar with all the statutory enactments and judicial decisions in addition to having a sound knowledge of the common-law. 

The first volume of "Real Property Law in New Jer sey" gives the lawyer a ready source of the law affecting this vital subject as it is in New Jersey today. There have been decisions in this State on almost every phase of this subject and this work enables the lawyer to readily ascertain the present law without long and often tedious research. It will no longer be necessary to examine the law of other jurisdictions for a precedent can be found for almost every phase of this law in New Jersey. 

The lawyers of this State have long felt a need for such a work as "Real Property Law in New Jersey" and because of the soundness of its presentation, its exhaustive examination of the law and its easy understanding, it is indispensible in the library of even the smallest law office. 

Newark, N. J. 

February 14th, 1946 

John J. McCloskey

IV

Preface 

Real property law, being so vast and comprehensive, acts as a basis, and is all inclusive of the majority of legal subjects. The average attorney's practice depends a great deal upon real estate. A knowledge and ability to refer quickly to our laws and decisions is necessary for a successful practice. This extends not only to present law, but such laws as existed during the period covered by the chain of title. In presenting this, the first volume of "Real Property Law in New Jersey", it is hoped that a long felt want is being supplied.

The practicing attorney, particularly if his clerkship did not entail any abstracting experience, may well recall the difficulties in reading his first title and the lack of his practical knowledge in the closing. The incompleteness of his law school training, the failure to find any New Jersey treatise or reference book which covered the great field of real property law made his task the more difficult. 

Many excellent volumes deal with the common law and early statutory law of the United States, but the rules of law of the various jurisdictions may not apply to our state, nor are changes in the law brought before the attention of the New Jersey practitioner.

In the conviction of the need of such a work, both as a treatise and ready reference, the publishers have exerted every effort to publish all the law in New Jersey, both statutory and judicial, which should be complete, compact, and which would act as a ready reference to those questions dealing with the law of real property. 

It is our purpose to cover this field as completely as possible and yet concisely enough so that whatever point is in question can be readily found. This in itself is not sufficient unless the point of law can be readily understood and differentiated. If by using these volumes one can learn, or find out what it is all about, that is all we can ask. If perchance we have also pointed out any weaknesses in law, or desirable reforms (such as encouraging a desire to make title good of record, instead of merely good and marketable) we shall be doubly grateful.

REAL PROPERTY LAW IN NEW JERSEY 

The language and style attempted to be employed is such as to be readily understood by the reader without debating the meaning of the writing as confusing the law. 

If the terseness and directness is the basis of criticism it must be remembered that, in the preparation, these volumes are meant for the practical, functional use of the practicing attorney. While primarily meant for the attorney, we also hope that the law school student and others with legal background or experience in real estate may find this useful and understandable.

REFERENCES 

Primarily all references to the law are based on New 

Jersey decisions or statutes. Where questions may have come up of which no New Jersey reference or decision by our courts have been found, or where common law principles have not been directly decided in New Jersey, for such evident omissions other sources have been used.

Our desire is to accomplish for lawyers time saving devices by accurate precedent quickly referred to. Thus the reader is saved much time and effort and can accomplish in a few minutes what has required the authors many hours of painstaking effort.

For purposes of quick reference there is also included a table of cases and one of the Revised Statutes. By referring to the pages in the text the cases and statutes are quickly bound up with other references.

TABLE OF CONTENTS AND INDEX 

In the use of books of this type the author has generally found it beneficial to refer first to the table of contents in order to obtain the broad outline of the volume and of the particular subjects involved. For more detailed reference the index is then used. The table of contents has been analytically devised so that the general subject may be found and the arrangement is attempted to be followed in logical sequence. For this purpose the table of contents refers to sections. The index refers to pages so that it should not be necessary to peruse a whole section, which may cover several pages, to find that item which is being looked for. In order further to simplify the search, under VI

PREFACE 

"W" there is included a Word and Phrase reference where one may look for a particular word which describes the point in question.

FORMS 

In the desire to make each volume complete in itself forms are included. An index to forms follows the formsand precedes the table of cases. It would be impossible to make a set of forms to cover all possible needs, but the forms, herein contained, covering the subjects of the volume should be comprehensive enough for general, practical needs.

SUCCEEDING VOLUMES 

The practical impossibility of including every subject which may have some reference to real property law should be recognized. In the attempt to make this complete as we can, there is now under preparation by various attorneys specializing in real property various other chapters to be included in subsequent volumes. This should unite these other subjects for a well rounded, all-inclusive treatise on real property so that this reference work will contain within its covers the answer to any real estate problem that may come up. 

Pocket parts are provided for future changes and further reference. 

If any omissions or errors are found it would be greatly appreciated if such would be called to our attention. We are also open to, and you are invited to submit any further suggestions or criticisms.

In closing, the author wishes to acknowledge the assistance and active cooperation of Mr. H. S. Rosenberg of the Associated Lawyers Publishing Co. who sponsored and conceived these volumes. Without his initiative and persistence this work would not have been possible. 

Sam J. Abraham 

Elizabeth, New Jersey 

February, 1946.

XVI

CHAPTER I  §1. Introductory. 

Since it is the sole purpose of these volumes to be of practical functional use for the practicing attorney, no detailed discussions of the philosophy, theory or history behind the principles involved will be included except in so far as it would furnish a background for the consideration of the subject being discussed. It will be sufficient for the purpose of this chapter to define property in its various forms as it would affect real estate and how the various factors have been determined by the courts of New Jersey; the types of estates and incidents pertaining to each. $2. Definition of Property.

The most common definition of property is that it is "the right and interest which one has in lands and chattels to the exclusion of others."¹

Blackstone defines property "as the sole and despotic dominion which one man claims and exercises over the external things of the world in total exclusion of the right of any other individual in the universe." " $2. Bouviers. 

2 Blackstone Com. 2. Property embraces every species of valuable right and interest-Bouviers. The elements of all property is the right to possess, use, enjoy and dispose of a thing, 1 Bla Com. 186. 1

§3. Kinds and Classes of Property. 

The first division is between real and personal property. Generally, and for our purposes, we will consider real property as immovable property and personal property as movable. The basis of this classification being the civil law distinction under the Feudal System between corporeal and incorporeal property.' Corporeal property; that which can be touched or seen, having been divided into movable property and immovable. Incorporeal property being things which might not be handled,* such as choses in action, easements, etc. 2 Property may also be divided into public and private property. Those divided among individuals become private property. Those things not divided among individuals and still belong to the state are called public property. Of public property some are reserved for the necessities of the state and are used for public benefit. Other public property remain common to all the citizens, who take of them and use them each according to his necessities, and according to the laws which regulate their use. 

This latter is called common property. Air, running water, wild beasts, etc., have been called common property. 1 §3. For a complete discussion reference is made to

$5. Chattels Real.

Chattels real are interests which are annexed to or concern real estate. Chattel interests in land are chattels which partake of the nature of real property and are considered personal property. So a leasehold is a chattel real whether the duration be for one, for ninety-nine or for nine hundred ninety-nine years,' and as such, will go to the executor or administrator as personal property. All interests for a shorter period than for life, or all interests for a definite space of time, measured by years, months or 1 $5. ¹ Hutchinson v. Bramhall, 42 E 372, 7 A 873 reversing 40 E 83, 2 A 292, (E&A). DeKyne v. Lewis, 139 A 434, 5 M 948. McCormick v. Stephany, 57 E 257, 41 A 840 aff'd 61 E 208, 48 A 25. Application for awarding of process subpoena pursuant to R. S. 54:4-16, 34 A 

2nd 239, 21 M 387. Although in an action for nuisance, the person holding possession under a lease for 999 years, (the structure had been erected by the predecessor in title which was continued), is for all practical purposes the owner and should be so considered, rather than a tenant for years-Black v. Delaware & Raritan Canal, 24 E 455. 

Meyer v. Harris, 6 L 83, 38 A 690, holds that such conveyance is substantially one in fee. This principal has also been followed where taxes were assessed against a lessee holding a 999 year lease, saying that a tenant for years has an interest in land, and is therefore an owner of property. 

Ordinarily an estate for years has no market value, but on a long term where the rent is grossly disproportionate to the value of the land, an estate for years has a market value-Ocean Grove Camp Meeting v. Reeves, 79 L 334, 75 A 782 affirmed 80 L 464, 79 A 1119.

A privilege to renew, and an unexercised option to purchase, contained in such a lease are also chattel interests. However, in the matter of taxation, it has been the practice to consider leaseholds as real property. Since rent is an incident to the reversion and follows it, arrears of rent are chattels real of a mixed nature being partly in possession and partly in action." $6. Crops and Trees.

Fructus naturales are products of the earth not requiring annual cultivation.¹ Fructus industriales are crops which require annual sowing or planting.' The former are the natural, permanent and spontaneous growth of the 2 At common law estates in fee, for life, or for years, livery of seizin was necessary (this relic of the feudal system still prevails in New Jersey), but leases for years might have been made by parol, without livery of seizin-Hutchinson v. Bramhall, Supra. 

McCormick v. Stephany, Supra. 

'R. S. 1:1-2. Application for awarding of process of subpoena pursuant to R. S. 54:4-16, Supra.

Condit v. Neighbor, 13 L 83. They can be severed by the act of the owner or by operation of law-Demars v. Koehler, 62 L 203, 41 A 720 reversing 60 L 314, 38 A 

808, 72 AS 642 (E&A). 432. 

86. I Slocum v. Seymour, 36 L 138, 13 Am Rep.

3 soil and are considered real property today as at common law. Therefore, a sale of standing timber is a sale of an interest in land and is required by the Statute of Frauds to be in writing. But where the vendor agrees to sever and sell the trees as timber, even though designated as being upon certain land, no property passes to the vendee until they are cut down and reduced to chattels. Then such a sale is of personalty and need not be in writing. The conversion in such a case from real property to personal property is a matter of intention.

Where timber is felled by trespassers, it is vested as personal property in the owner of the fee, and at his death goes to his personal representative and not to his heirs." 

The same is true when it is cut by a tenant for life or less estate, in which case it vests as personal property in the reversion. Manure lying in the barnyard, where it has accumulated is personal property. After it is spread on the land and appropriated to fertilizing purposes it becomes part of the freehold."

Where a trunk of a tree is standing on land belonging to two people, they are tenants in common of the un-

divided one-half, no matter how slight the proportion of the trunk is on the other land." In case there are overhanging branches, the fruit or the branches may be cut, but they must be returned to the owner of the trunk, or such person will be guilty of conversion." $7. Growing Crops.

The distinction between perennial products of the earth and the annual fruits of annual labor is that in the latter they are regarded as chattels¹ in that title may pass by a parol sale; they may be taken in execution against the chattels, and they pass to the administrator and not the heir. But in a conveyance of the real estate whether by the owner or by the sheriff, under a sale by judgment and execution, growing crops are considered annexed to the soil so that they pass by deed as an incident to the real estate,* unless there is an express reservation in the deed. Where the owner of the soil sells the growing crops as chattels, such a sale operates as a severance, (or the purchase of the growing crops under an execution sale) and such purchaser may leave the crops upon the soil until its maturity and

At common law emblements was the right of a tenant holding a term of uncertain duration to come back after his term and remove the crops planted by him before the end of the term. This right applied where the term ended either by act of God or by operation of law.¹ As to such tenant emblements are personal property. This rule has been relaxed so that the tenant has the right even where his term is for a time certain.2 But if the estate is determined by the act of the tenant, through his own fault or misconduct, between seed time and harvest, his right to emblements is destroyed and the growing crop

A fixture is an article which was a chattel, but which, by being fixed to the realty, becomes accessory to it and part of it.¹ At common law, whatever was affixed to the soil became a part of it and was consequently subject

to the same rights of property as the soil itself." The common law rule has been greatly relaxed and the degree of annexation and the resultant injury to the freehold by detachment has become of increasing importance. $10. Tests. §11. Annexation. 

.

In order to constitute chattels as fixtures they must be actually annexed to the building or something appurtenant to it, and they need not necessarily be attached to the building.¹ Permanent is as that word is used in business transactions, permanent as contradistinguished from temporary. It is not necessary that there be an intention to make the annexation perpetual.³ 2 The permanency of the annexation does not depend on the degree of physical force with which the thing is attached, and the annexation may be slight. Where the personal property is so incorporated as to lose its identity, and therefore, not separable without material injury to the freehold, or to the article itself, then it becomes part of the realty by being absorbed in it and is no longer a chattel." $11. The above rules are differently applied as the question may arise between landlord and tenant, heir and executor, mortgagor and mortgagee, etc.¹ And the rule as to the right to sever and remove fixtures is stronger as between mortgagor and mortgagee than as between landlord and tenant or other relation. As between mortgagor and mortgagee, heir and executor, etc., where there is some permanent 2 Although an innocent purchaser of the realty, without notice, would not be affected by such agreement that the article shall retain its personal character, and be subject to removal-Ivins v. Ackerson, 38 L 220, James Leo Co. v. Jersey City Bill Posting Co., Supra. If this were not so it would do away with the purposes of the recording acts and otherwise would seriously endanger the rights of purchasers, afford opportunities for frauds, and introduce uncertainty and confusion in land titles. $14. 'Potts v. N. J. Arms & Ord. Co., 17 E 395, affirmed 17 E 516; Rogers v. Brokaw, 25 E 496, affirmed 26 E 563. The rules with regard to fixtures has been much relaxed as between tenant for life and remainderman and also as between landlord and tenant. But as between heir and executor, grantor and grantee, mortgagor and mortgagee, the rule has undergone no change- 

Crane v. Brigham, 11 E 29. Quimby v. Manhattan Cloth & Paper Co., 24 E 260. 

2 Rogers v. Brokaw, Supra. 18

attachment that is necessary for the enjoyment of the real estate, its character as a fixture is determined as above set forth. As between landlord and tenant, the question is whether the tenant made the improvement for the benefit of his term and not for the reversion.*

As between vendor and vendee and mortgagor and mortgagee, etc., anything of a permanent nature provided by the owner, which is fitted for and actually applied to the use of the premises, and annexed, becomes part of the realty. Such items as keys, fire frames, doors, window blinds, double windows pass with the realty, 1 Washb. 14, 15. Even trade fixtures erected by the owner pass with the grant, §15, n. 11. In Erdman v. Moore, 58 L 445, 33 A 

958, a portable furnace and a portable range were held part of the realty even though the connection was of slightest character. In Cunningham v. Seaboard Realty, 67 E 210, 58 A 918, gas logs, chandeliers and window screens in a residence were fixtures. A warm air furnace was a fixture in Workingmen's B. & L. Ass'n v. Del 

Vecchio, 151 A 282, 8 M 563. In going over these cases it must not be forgotten that each case must be determined according to its own particular facts, §2, n. 4. In Quimby v. Manhattan Cloth & Paper Co. 24 E 260, boilers, engines and shafting were considered part of the building as between the mortgagor and mortgagee. In Rogers v. 

Brokaw, 25 E 496 affirmed 26 E 563, held that movable machinery was different in nature and legal character from steam engines, boilers and other articles secured by masonry or other substantial annexation. Keeler v. Keeler, 31 E 181, also held that steam engines, boilers, shafting, belting, pulleys, water wheels, governor and gas generator were part of the realty. But cotton machinery such 19

as spinning frames fastened to the floor by nails and screws were personal property. 

The leading case of Feder v. Van Winkle 53 E 370, 33 A 399, 51 AS 628, (E&A), held that heavy machinery and appliances were unified with the land and incorporated together as a whole. The fact that they could be removed and utilized elsewhere, did not change the character. This case limits Rogers v. Brokaw, 25 E 496 affirmed 26 E 

563, and distinguishes it in that the machinery in the Rogers case was movable and was moved about at the convenience of the owner. 

In Penn Ins. Co. v. Semple, 38 E 575, (E&A), machines were also fastened by small screws and were moved about from one place of the building to the other and the court could find no evidence showing a design on the part of the owners to make a permanent addition to the realty. In Speiden v. Parker, 46 E 292, 19 A 21, (E&A), the business was a quarry and dock. The boiler, engine, etc. were fastened to the ground and the railroad track and other machinery were used for the only purpose to which the real estate was put. Implements, however, were not specially adapted and could have been removed and applied to the use of a like business elsewhere. Lee v. Hubschmidt, 55 E 623, was concerned with a general building and woodworking business. The machines were not moved from place to place and they were held fixtures as against the contention that they were light affairs and that the fastening was simply to keep them steady to prevent them from jarring or jumping about when in motion. The inference that they were fixtures overcame the intention. 20

In Crane Iron Works v. Wilkes, 64 L 193, 45 A 1033, as to the mortgagee, the machines in the machine shop were capable of being used wherever power can be supplied and were fastened only by being screwed to the floor to keep it steady while in operation. It was held that this was not part of the realty. General Electric Co. v. Transit 

Equip. Co. 57 E 460, 42 A 101, held an electric generator and switchboard not a fixture as between the mortgagee and conditional vendor. 

In the case of Atlantic Safe Deposit & Trust Co. v. 

Atlantic City Laundry Co., 64 E 140, 53 A 212, the engine, boiler and wash machine were part of the real estate. But the office furniture, clothes press, soap kettle, etc., in the laundry plant, which were not attached to the building, constituted personal property. In the Knickerbocker 

Trust Co. v. Penn Cordage Co., case (58 A 408, 66 E 

305, 105 Am. Rep. 640, (E&A), reversing 62 E 624, 50 A 

459), the machinery was fastened to the floor by adjustable attachments and held to be personal property in that the machinery could be removed without injury either to themselves or to the building in which they were located. The reversal of the lower court as to certain items which were not annexed to the building. Lifschitz v. Vorclone Corp. 148 A 899, 8 M 83, was a case of the seller against the owner and it was held that the machine resting on the floor with screws and with removable connections to the steam pipe and also some shafting annexed to the wall by screws was not affixed to the realty. In 

McDonald v. H. B. McDonald Const. Co., 117 E 181, 175

A 87, a sand and gravel washing plant which could be dismantled and taken down in sections was held to be no part of the realty. Chancellor v. Cruze, 83 E 232, 90 A 21

Conditional Sales Act.

The Conditional Sales Act requires the separate filing of conditional sale contracts where the goods are affixed to the realty in certain cases: 

1. Where the goods are not severable without material injury to the freehold, the reservation of title is void as against any person who has not expressly assented to the reservation. 

2. If they are severable, without material injury to the freehold, the reservation of title is void as against subsequent purchasers of the realty unless the contract is filed. sequently accepted a new lease from the purchaser without excepting or reserving to himself the building he erected to himself. It was held that he could not successfully assert any title to the building. 

14 Greenspan-Greenberger Co. v. The Goerke Co., Supra. 15 

Torrey & Torrey v. Burnett, Supra-In this case there was an agreement which the landlord failed to perform; the tenant was given a reasonable time to remove such fixtures although his term was ended and possession surrendered, 26

3. As against the owner of the realty the seller's reservation is void unless the contract is filed before the goods are affixed.

The Conditional Sales Act did not change the basic law of fixtures except as against third parties (judgment creditors, subsequent purchasers and mortgagees) and the rights of immediate parties, to control the question as between themselves by agreement, remain as before. This rule, however, is subject to the proviso that the chattel is not so closely incorporated into the realty as substantially to lose its identity or to be incapable of removal without substantial injury to the freehold or to the article itself." 

The act alters the law of fixtures in cases of conditional sales that intent determines whether chattels affixed to realty become fixtures. The purpose of this section of the conditional Sales Act is to give actual or constructive notice." $16. R. S. 46:32-14. ? Bank of America Nat. Ass'n v. LaReine Hotel, 108

$17. Institutional Doctrine.

The institutional doctrine is based only on the rule of adaptability and disregards the other requisites of intention of the parties, and of the character of the annexation of the fixtures. The interpretation of the courts disregards the relationship of the parties involved and seems to follow the common law rule of fixtures as between owner and purchaser. 1 The institutional doctrine has been applied only to cases involving conditional bills of sale and is an outgrowth of this act. The rule is accepted law in New Jersey although this does not seem to have been followed in other jurisdictions or in the federal courts.2

The doctrine has been defined as follows: "When a chattel which, clearly, is permanently essential to the completeness of the structure having regard to the character of that structure and the functioning of it in the use for which it was obviously designed, is actually, purposely, and lawfully affixed to and into the structure, it becomes a part of the realty" ³ This principle has caused some confusion in its application and the interpretation 1 

817. Preceding section. 2 

See dissenting opinion in Smyth Sales Corp. v. Norfolk B. & L. Ass'n, 116 L 293, 184 A 204, (E&A). 

This quotation is from Smyth Sales Corp. v. Norfolk B. & L. Ass'n, Supra. The theory is based on the leading case of Feder v. Van Winkle, 53 E 370, 33 A 399, 51 AS 628, and the decisions as interpreted in Chancellor 28

of particular matters may be highly speculative. Further clarification of the practical application and express declarations of the precedents which are and which are not to be followed are desirable and necessary. 

The theory of the doctrine is that the degree of affixation is less important than the indispensibility of that which is affixed. This doctrine has been variously applied v. Cruze, 83 E 232, 90 A 673, Temple Co. v. Penn Mutual, 69 L 36, 54 A 295, and Knickerbocker Trust Co. v. Penn 

Cordage Co. 62 E 624, 50 A 459 reversed 66 E 305, 58 A

In Equity money has sometimes the incidents and attributes of real estate and vice versa. This is based on the equitable maxim that equity regards things which are supposed to be done, as having actually been performed, where nothing has intervened which ought to prevent such performance. Such consideration is left to texts on wills and administration.5 $20. Brief Background of the Feudal System.

In order to understand our modern system, the terms used in ownership of real property; and in order to intelligently apply the method of acquiring and transferring real estate, it is necessary to know the origin and history of the Feudal System. This will be done briefly. For a fuller discussion, reference is made to "Washburn's Real 

Property." 1

When the Western empire fell in the hands of the Teutonic Tribes, the lands were reduced to feuds as under the Roman Law. The King reserved an extensive propor tion of the lands for himself and parcelled the remainder out to those immediately under him. They in turn, subdivided the land, each to their respective groups. The system was primarily a military one, and the land was the reward, given for military services rendered to the King. 

This apportionment was made to the different tribes and cases cited. Camden Trust Co. v. Haldeman, 133 E 427, 33 A2d 611 and cases cited. Bostock v. Denim, 134 E 

388, 36 A2d 10. §443. 

See Clapp's "Wills and Administration" §431 to $20. Chapter 2. 36

accordingly, therefore, to dukes, marquis and barons, to whom the feuds were first granted.

In the eleventh century, this system was introduced to England after William the Conqueror invaded England in 1066. The basis of the Feudal System brought about three changes in the ownership of real property: 

1. The relationship of the King was established to all the land in the country, the use of which, was granted out by him to others. 

2. This created the basis for the doctrine of tenure which gradually developed. Tenure implied not only the actual holding of the land, but also the terms upon which the land was held. 

3. It gave rise to the Manorial System. 

Elements of. 

Ownership always remained in the King who granted the use to others whose obligation was to render some return for the privilege of holding. Therefore, ownership could not be absolute or outright. The fiefs, feuds or fees were created by such lord and could be destroyed by the one creating it. In addition, there was always the requirement of taking an oath of fealty to the landlord, without which no feud could subsist. Originally this gift or "benefice" was not hereditary.2 

2 The Right of Inheritance was created by customary law. Under feudal law the sons were recognized as heirs, and, at first all took equally. Later, because of the military feuds, the eldest alone took to the exclusion of others, hence, the doctrine of primogeniture. It was not until 37

Tenancies. 3 The King was the lord paramount and the ones to whom the crown granted the land were mesne, or middle lords, (barons) or tenants in capi:e; the others were tenants paravail and then came the vassals and sub-vassals, each holding through some superior. There were the military tenants who held by Knight-Service, and there were the socage tenants. A tenure by free and common socage was where the tenant paid a fixed rent, usually by giving a share of the crops or cattle. From this free and common socage, the freehold estate was developed. 

A tenure by Villein socage was paid by certain specified labor in lieu of a fixed rental. But a tenure by pure villeinage was where the tenant's obligations were not specified and he did not know what type of work he would be called upon to do. Such tenant could be taxed at the will of the lord.

In addition to services incident to tenure, there also grew up the right of wardship and marriage, which also embraced the custody of the minor. A refusal to carry out the lord's bargain of marriage, caused the infant to forfeit the value of such marriage, and if the infant married without consent, then the forfeiture was double that amount. the Statute of Westminster the Second, that these estates became subject to debts. "The words used were dedi and concessi (I have given and granted), from which source we retain "given and granted" in deeds. "There were fixed and ascertained as were the services of a tenure by villein socage. 38

The lord, on the other hand, was under no obligation to account for the profit of the land.

A relief was similar to a tax where the fief or feud descended, which the tenant paid to the lord on coming into possession or taking up possession. This grew into the payment for the privilege of exercising the right of alienation of property which was called a fine. In addition, another incident of tenure was escheat, in which the feud returned to the lord's hands by a termination of the tenure for failure, crimes, etc. Other burdens were aides, to ransom a lord when he was a prisoner, for money to make the eldest son a knight, or for money to marry the eldest daughter."

Primer seizin applied only where the tenant held directly from the crown. Upon the death of the Middle 

Lord, if his son (the heir of the tenant in capite) wanted to succeed him, he had to pay primary seizin, which was usually the amount equivalent to one year's profits. 

There was also a tenancy of Frankalmoign, where lands were held by the church, consideration for which was religious services to the grantor. Grand Sergeanty was land held directly from the crown in consideration of personal and honorable services to the King. 

Freehold Estates.

Freehold estates have their origin in tenure by free and common socage. Blackstone defines a freehold estate, 'as any estate of inheritance or for life, in either corporeal .. 

5 These other burdens were abolished by the Statute of Charles the Second, as were military tenures. 39

of incorporeal hereditament, existing in or arising from real property in free tenure." At common law, a freehold estate was created by the ceremony of feoffment and delivery of seizin. The feoffor was the grantor, the feoffee was the grantee, and the feoffment was the formal delivery of seizin by ceremony. The formal part of the ceremony was called the investiture. This required, in the presence of witnesses, the going upon the land and the owner of the property giving the feoffee actual possession by putting into his hand some part of the premises, like a twig or branch. Technically, this was the livery of seizin and signified the completion of the Feudal Investiture.' Sub-infeudation.

Sub-infeudation was the greatest evil of the Feudal System. This was the means whereby the vassal, instead of alienating the land or transferring the tenure, created a new and inferior feud out of the one he had. By reason of this the lord could not collect from the transferee, since the transferee owed his duty to his transferor. In the 

Magna Charta of 1215, the sub-infeudation of the entire feud was prohibited and the vassal was compelled to retain enough of it to secure the services due to the lord. In 

1290, the Statute Quia Emptores, was passed, the main purpose of which was to abolish the sub-infeudation of estates in fee simple. Under this Statute, the one convey- 

2 Bla. Com. 104. 'The term seizin always has reference to a legal title -Disborough v. Outcalt, 1 E 298. Seizin in fact, requires an actual possession of the land. A seizin in law gives the right to immediate possession and can be con- 40

ing lost all his interest in the estate, and his grantee held directly from the original lord. This statute was the first creation of the fee simple estate as we know today. 

Allodial System.

The Allodial System of tenure is of absolute ownership. In theory, it is that all property is held from the state, and the individual holds the property subject to the sovereign power, which in turn, is subject to constitutional limitations. The Allodial System of tenure has prevailed in the United States since the Revolutionary War.'

Under this, a fee simple is an outright estate giving absolute ownership. $21. Law in Force.

Title to real estate and the incidents of ownership are still governed by the common law except where the comverted to one in fact. Since seizin necessarily implies possession, the law presumes possession to be according to his title or color of title. Therefore anyone claiming adversely to one in possession under color of title must prove a better title in order to justify disturbing his possession. Seizin cannot be lost to a stranger. Hill v. Carter, 16 L 87. Possession is title, technically speaking, of a low grade, it is true, but still it is title. He who has possession may fold his arms in security, until his right is impeached.

mon law has been repealed or altered by the constitution or by legislative authority.' And full consideration is given to the English decisions and rules prior to the Revolution, but not after.2 The intent to alter common law principles cannot be inferred and must be clearly expressed and then no more than the case absolutely requires. $22. Kinds of Estates.

An estate pertaining to real property is technically the degree, quantity, nature and extent of interest which a person has in real property.' Such estates may be divided: 

1. In reference to its duration or extent of time, the quantity of interest. This in turn is divided into freehold estates and estates less than freehold.2 

2. In reference to the circumstances under which it may be held and enjoyed, whether in severalty or in connection with others. A tenant in severalty is a sole tenant, one who holds real property in his own right alone. Estates in plurality are joint tenancies, estates by the entirety, tenancies in common, tenancies in partnership. 

3. The qualification of interest whether the estate is absolute or conditional-uses, trusts and conditions.*

The conditional estates are divided into conditions precedent and conditions subsequent. The determination of which is meant is based upon the intention of the parties.

Fee Tail Estates. 

In a technical sense "conditional fee" was held to apply to those estates which the statute de donis conditionalibus, (also known as Statute of Entails) was construed by the courts as fee tail estates.18 These are estates of inheritance which descend to the heirs of the donee's body, instead of to the heirs generally. This means his lawful issue, his children, and through them to his grandchildren in a direct line so long as his posterity endures in a regular order and course of descent, and upon the death of the first owner without issue, the estate determines.¹9 

17 Cases cited, n. 12, Supra.

Such an estate was treated as one with a condition subsequent, and the lords lost the right to escheat. By a fictitious action brought by a third person the tenant was able to change his fee tail into a fee-simple. 

This would be, usually, an ejectment action in which the tenant would suffer a fine. By permission of the court the litigating parties entered into a final agreement on the record, which was binding upon them like any judgment, and the claimant's right to the land was admitted and established. Common recovery was also a fictitious suit brought by one who was to purchase the estate against the tenant in tail who was willing to convey."

Conventional Life Estates 

A conventional life estate may be an estate to the tenant for his own life, or for the life of some other person, called an estate per autre vie.5 $26. Conveyance by Life Tenant.

A tenant for life is the owner of an independent estate and, unless restrained by the term of his grant, he may convey his entire interest, or underlet the whole or any part of the estate for a term not exceeding that of his own.¹ At common law if the tenant for life attempted to convey a greater estate than he had, such an act would work a forfeiture and the tenant would lose his interest in the entire estate. Now, by the statute of uses, the conveyance would only extend to the interest which he alone has and it would not work a forfeiture.* 2

A subsequent chapter¹ treats of the relationship of landlord and tenant. Therefore, at this point, it is sufficient to state that estates less than freehold are (1) tenancies for a term certain, which term is specified; (2) periodical tenancies, such as year to year, and month to month; (3) tenancies at will; and (4) tenancies at sufferance. $30. Estates in Plurality. 

A tenancy in plurality is distinguished from one in severalty in that more than one person holds the property, and the right of enjoyment can be exercised by several.¹ 

Estates in plurality are the joint estates, joint tenancies, and tenancies by the entirety; tenancies in common; estates in coparcenary;2 and tenancies in partnership. $31. Partnership Property.

Partnership real estates is considered personal property for all purposes.¹ The survivor is a trustee in respect

Where two or more persons form one heir. At common law when there were no sons, the property would descend to the females and was called an estate in coparcenary. Under our Statute of Descent, males and females take equally as tenants in common. 60

The essential feature of a tenancy in common is unity of possession.¹ A joint tenancy requires, in addition to the unity of possession, three other unities, the unity of time, of title, and of interest.2 Between tenants in common there is no privity of estate. The unity of interest in joint tenancies is per my et per tout while a tenant in common simply holds an undivided share. On the decease of one joint tenant the survivor holds the whole property by virtue of the original grant, and no part of it is held in anywise under the decedent. Such right of survivorship is called the jus accrescendi. 1 $31. R. S. 46:3-17. R. S. 42:1-25 and 26-Before

At common law where an estate was conveyed to two or more persons, without indicating how the property was to be held, a joint tenancy was created. Since 1812, by statute all estates to two or more persons are tenancies in common, unless expressly declared to be joint tenancies by the deed or instrument creating them.' 

This statute is applicable to estates for years, and to life estates. $33. Joint Tenancies and Estates by the Entirety Distinguished.

Where husband and wife have title as tenants by the entirety, they hold possession between themselves as tenants in common during their joint lives, with remainder to the survivor.¹ As such, they have equal rights as to the profits and use of the property.2 Either one is entitled to an accounting of the rents collected from the common §34. 1 Zubler v. The rule that neither can dispose nor convey does not apply to the interest which one has. Thus one may sell, mortgage, or otherwise alienate all his interest in the estate for life which he holds in common with the other." Although such a conveyance cannot operate to let in a third party as a tenant by the entirety with the other, such third party would become a tenant in common for the joint lives of both husband and wife. One also has the power to sell, subject to the contingency of the other predeceasing him, and such conveyance becomes absolute if he survives.

$35. Relationship of Co-Tenants to One Another. 

Share of Interest.

In the absence of an agreement to the contrary, each co-tenant is presumed to hold a share in the property according to his contribution.¹ Since one tenant in common usually holds possession for all, title by adverse possession cannot be asserted against the other.2 

Trust and Confidence.

A relationship of trust and confidence is implied where the cotenancy has its origin in a single title, or if they are in joint possession.2ª But where the cotenants are not in joint possession, acquired their titles at different times by different conveyances, such relationship of mutual trust and confidence does not exist, as a matter their title Sharbaro v. Sharbaro, 88 E 101, 102 A 256.

of law. Where such relationship does exist there is an implied obligation on each to sustain and protect the common title. Therefore, if such cotenant purchases that property, either directly or indirectly, at a foreclosure, or other judicial sale, or purchases an outstanding title, the purchase will be deemed to have been made for the benefit of all the cotenants. " Under this Statute the rule is that where the tenant in possession has prevented his cotenants from obtaining from the premises such profits as they were capable of yielding, or has taken possession of the whole and used them as his own, and thereby made a profit, he must account either for their fair rental value or the profits. Where part of the premises is occupied by a tenant in common, and he has received a profit from the rest of the property he is accountable for that profit. He is also accountable for the value of the use of that part occupied by him if his conduct is such as to exclude his cotenants from their right to the enjoyment of the property.º property was a rear lot not capable of joint occupancy.

$37. Repairs & Expenses. 

In general tenants in common are equally obligated for taxes, insurance, water, rents and repairs and there is no distinction between such duties of a tenant in common with that of a life tenant.¹ To compel contribution of a proportionate share of the repairs, where the expenses are paid by one, a request to unite in such repairs and a refusal must be shown.2

Where one cotenant in common is in sole possession of the common property he is under an obligation to make needful, ordinary repairs, and pay the taxes, mortgage interest and insurance premiums. CHAPTER II  

$38. Definition.

Dower has been defined as "the provision which the law makes for a widow out of the lands or tenements of her husband for her support and the nurture of her children." 1 

A dower right is a life tenancy of that share of real estate granted to the widow by statute for the benefit of the widow during her life time.2

Before the passage of the Dower Act in England, there were five species of dower. In New Jersey, by the 

1927 Amendment, the dower right was increased from 1/3 to ½ and the statute now reads as follows: "The widow, whether alien or not, of a person dying intestate or otherwise, shall be endowed for the term of her natural life, of the one full and equal

Wheeler v. Kirtland, 27 E 534 (E&A).

half part of all real estate whereof her husband, or another to his use, was seized of an estate of inheritance at any time during coverture, to which she shall not have relinquished or released her right of dower by deed duly executed and acknowledged in the manner provided by law for deeds to be recorded."

This amendment operates only on land of which the husband became seized after the act went into effect, January 1, 1929.5 As to land in which seisin during overthe church-door to be married, endowed his wife a certain portion of his lands. c. Dower ex assensu patris, which differed from dower ad ostium ecclesiae only in being made out of the lands of the husband's father and with his consent. d. Dower de la plus belle, where the widow, on suing the guardian in chivalry for dower, was required by him to endow herself of the fairest portion of any lands she might hold as guardian in socage, and thus release from dower the lands of her husband held in chivalry. This was abolished along with the military tenures, of which it was a consequence. e. Dower by common law, where the widow was entitled during her life to a third part of all the lands and tenements of which her husband was seized in law or in fact of an inheritable estate, at any time during the coverture, and which any issue she might have had might by possibility have inherited. It was afterwards confirmed by Magna Charta and some limit was imposed on the power to defeat its consummation. 4 R. S. 3:37-1. 76

ture took place, before January 1, 1929, this right is determined by the law in force prior thereto." $39. Curtesy.

At common law, before the birth of issue alive, the husband had an estate of freehold in his wife's land during their joint lives. After issue was born alive, capable of inheriting the estate, the husband acquired an estate of freehold which continued for his own life. This was termed an estate by the "curtesy initiate" during his wife's life, and it became consummate upon the death of the wife. 

The Married Woman's Act, passed in 1852, deprived the husband of the freehold during his wife's life and he no longer held a freehold estate in possession. The Married 

Woman's Act did not affect the estate which he took after her death. Upon that event, he became entitled to a freehold interest in possession for his own life.¹ 

In 1927, "An act relative to curtesy" was passed which took effect January 1, 1929. This act increased the right, given by the 1915 act from one-third to one-half and removed the common

This amendment also applies only to land wherein the seisin during coverture commenced after the date specified for the statute to take effect, that is, after January 1, 1929.³

The statute pertaining to curtesy now reads as follows: 

"The widower, whether alien or not, of a person dying intestate or otherwise, shall be endowed, for the term of his natural life, of the one full and equal half part of all real estate whereof his wife, or another to her use, was seized of an estate of inheritance at any time during coverture, whether issue be born or not, to which he shall not have relinquished or released his right of curtesy by deed duly executed and acknowledged in the manner provided by law for deeds to be recorded, which shall be known as curtesy and which right of curtesy shall be enforced, admeasured and determined in the same maner and according to the same procedure and subject to the same limitations. and restrictions as is provided by law in case of dower." 4

The effect of these amendments were to put curtesy on exactly the same footing as dower," except that there seems to be no change in the section pertaining to jointure. 

As used in the balance of this chapter the word "dower" will include and can be used interchangeably with "curtesy." Thus, the word "curtesy" may be substituted for the word "dower" wherever it may appear, and the gender interchanged, except when reference is made to the status of dower or curtesy before January 1, 1929. $40. Classification

There are three classifications of dower or curtesy: 

1. So long as both spouses live it is inchoate. 

2. If the owner predeceases the other, the right becomes consummate. 

3. Upon entering into possession by assignment or admeasurement, under the statute, the estate becomes absolute and complete. $41. Inchoate Right.

The inchoate right of dower or curtesy, ". . . is a present, fixed, and vested valuable interest of one spouse " Stabel v. Gertel, Supra. Walker v. Bennett, Supra. 

R. S. 3:38-20 says that the right of curtesy "may be enforced and admeasured in the same manner and according to the same procedure and subject to the same limitations and restrictions, as is provided in this article in the case of dower." $57. 79

in the other's estate of inheritance in land of which such other spouse is seized, defeasable upon predeceasing such other spouse." ." The right is something more than a mere possibility and while not technically an estate, it is still a valuable interest in the land.2 It becomes coincident with the seisin of the husband. The estate is vested in interest during the lifetime of the ultimate beneficiary. Once it attaches by seisin of the husband, it cannot be discharged by any act of the owner or grantee whether done in good faith or fraudulently. It is in no sense her husband's property, but hers alone given to her as against her husband, and all those claiming §41. ¹Class v. Strack, 85 E 319, 96 A 405. Gerhardt v. Kind of Estate.

The right to dower and curtesy are favored in law.³ 

It is a freehold estate in possession. Since the estate becomes vested in interest during the lifetime of the ultimate beneficiary, it is not an estate by descent.5 Such an estate is paramount to the title of a purchaser at a sheriff's sale on execution against him, and it is also paramount to a legacy reduced to a judgment, even though the lands were charged with payment of legacy. Equitable Estates.

The doctrine of courts of equity is that equitable estates are considered, to all intents and purposes, as legal estates. They are subject to the same incidents, properties and consequences as under like circumstances belong to similar estates at law.¹ Our statute on dower enlarged the common law right to include ... or another to his use, was seized of an estate of inheritance," and the widow is now entitled to dower in an equitable estate of her husband the same as in his legal estate. In equity actual possession 2 ee

of the estate, or the receipt of rents, or where he is entitled to a conveyance of the legal estate, or possession by the trustee for his benefit, is such seisin of the equitable estate as is equivalent to legal seisin. By the doctrine of equitable conversion a contract to purchase land, on the death of the vendee, descends to his heirs, and the executor or administrator is to discharge the contract out of the personal estate, therefore, the widow is entitled to dower in the property which was the subject matter of the contract.* 

A limitation in the deed, grant or devise will not exclude the right of dower." Thus, where land is held in trust for the separate use of one of the spouses, she is entitled to dower, and the fact that one spouse derived this title from the other does not exclude this right. who was to hold the property for the sole benefit of the husband and his heirs, free from the wife's dower or right of dower, which right she expressly released, it was held that the deed of itself raised an equitable estate in fee in the husband. As to this estate the deed was not effective as a release of her dower."

In such cases, where the land is held in trust, and the use appears of record, any purchaser takes with notice of the wife's right of dower, and, as such, she has no standing in equity to impress it on the title.10 But where the conveyance is made to defraud the wife and it does not appear of record, equity will grant relief.¹¹ 

Equity of Redemption.

In the case of an equity of redemption, the mortgagor is the legal owner as against all except the mortgagee and dower attaches to the equity of redemption.12 Such equity

of redemption is the right to relieve the land from the incumbrance and be restored to the possession and enjoyment in fee.13 No dower will attach where the mortgage was executed and delivered before coverture and the equity of redemption has been united by a release or conveyance to the legal title of the mortgagee. But where the purchaser of the equity of redemption took an assignment of the mortgage, dower attaches subject to the mortgage.

Morris, Supra, the purchaser of the equity of redemption, on an execution sale against the husband, paid off a mortgage given prior to the marriage. The purchaser took no assignment, but cancelled the mortgage of record. This was considered a voluntary payment of the mortgage debt and an extinguishment of the debt, and, as such, deprived him of all defense at law against the claim of dower. If the purchaser had taken an assignment and foreclosed, this would have been a valid defense to an action of dower. 

In equity, the wife was entitled to dower subject to the mortgage debt. Since she could not enforce her right against the purchaser, she had the right to redeem. To the same effect see Eldridge v. Eldridge, 14 E 195. See $49 infra.

$44. Quarantine. "Until dower or curtesy is assigned, the widow or widower may remain in, hold and enjoy the mansion house of his or her spouse and the messuage or plantation belonging thereto, without being liable to pay rent therefor. "After assignment of dower or curtesy, the rights confirmed in and granted to the widow or widower by this section shall cease." 1

Where there is no real estate, or dower to be assigned, then the right to retain possession does not attach.2 The right of quarantine gives the right to retain possession until assignment. This possession is an incident only to her dower, belonging to that right and inseparable from it. It is a privilege preceding and in no wise prevents or impedes the assignment or disposal of dower. The object is to give her a home, and the right of possession is extended until $44. 'R. S. 3:37-4. Before William the Conqueror, a widow was entitled to continue in the principal messuage of her husband for the term of one year after his death. 

During this period, by C7 of the Magna Charta, an assignment was made to her of her dower. This privilege was called the "Widow's Quarantine." The time was limited to forty days and the heir was still bound to assign the dower within this period-Den v. Dodd, 6 L 367. 

Woodruff v. Brown, 17 L 246. 2 

Morgan v. Titus, 3 E 201. In re. Howe's Estate, 112 E 17, 163 A 234. 

3 Bleecker v. Hennion, 23 E 123, says that the purpose of this section is to confer a right of possession similar to an estate or tenancy at will and not to create a life 93

her dower is assigned; and, as such, acts as a compulsion on the heir to make the assignment. 

The right is either to occupy, without any obligation to pay rent, or rent the homestead at her election. When the surviving spouse is out of possession, then dower (or curtesy) must be assigned before the surviving spouse can legally enter.' If the surviving spouse gives up the mansion and plantation, her right of entry is gone and she cannot re-enter. Her remedy is by action for dower. When she remains in possession, it is presumed to be possession under quarantine until dower has been assigned. Being in possession is a good bar to an action for ejectment,¹º and she cannot be ousted by the heirs or those claiming under the estate. Spinning v. Spinning, 41 E 427, 5 A 278 affirmed

When the widow is in possession, under quarantine, she is not chargeable with the maintenance of the property until dower is assigned.16 She is not a tenant for life and is not, therefore, bound to keep down interest on an incumbrance, nor to pay taxes, nor to make the necessary annual repairs.¹7 She is entitled to the profits undiminished by any such set offs¹8 and on her right to the rents, where she does not occupy the homestead, the one who does receive the rents is to be regarded as bailiff or trustee for her.¹9 $45. Assignment.

There is no estate in dower until actual assignment or admeasurement of that portion of lands to which her estate is to apply.' It is only a mere chose in action.²

"thing in action," it becomes applicable to the payments of her debts and a receiver may be appointed to have dower assigned for the benefit of her creditors.¹ Before dower has been assigned, the widow retains the right to have it assigned and once assignment has been made, she retains the part set off to her." Before her dower is set off, a widow is seized of no legal estate that can by her deed be vested in a stranger as against the heir at law. She can only convey, release or bar her dower interest to a terre tenant or to one in privity of estate. In equity, however, a chose in action is assignable and as to creditors, assignees and a mortgage by a dowress of her unassigned dower, equitable principles apply. In equity her dower right is also barred where she joins with the heirs in a mortgage on the principle of equitable estoppel. Before assignment, she cannot enter into possession nor maintain ejectment.

An action for dower can only be maintained against the owner of the land, or the tenant of the freehold, and no other person has the power to make a valid assignment.21 

A writ of dower will not lie against a person holding a mere chattel interest, or against one having an estate of less duration than the life of the dowress.22

The same rule applies where suit is brought against her—her dower is lost by default and such default is not prejudicial to her.³¹ $46. Damages.

The right to recover damages is given by statute as follows: 

"A widow or widower deforced of dower or curtesy in any real estate, or who cannot have it without suit, or whose right is unfairly assigned, or is not assigned within forty days after the death of his or her spouse, may recover the same together with damages sustained up until judgment by reason of the use and enjoyment thereof being unlawfully withheld, but as to real estate whereof his or her spouse was seized but not seized at death, damages shall be recovered only from such time as he or she shall make demand upon the owner for dower or curtesy therein." ¹

Deforcement is the wrongful withholding or the denial of the widow's right." Since the duty is placed on the heirs, a plea by the defendant that he was always ready to perform (tout temps prist), is not sufficient to bar damages for the detention of dower. Where the husband did not die seized of the lands, then the widow is entitled to dower only from the time of demand. If no demand is made, then damages may be recovered only from the time of filing the bill. The damages are the right to mesne profits accruing from the time of death (or demand) to the time of assignment." days after the death of the husband. Statute of Merton, the widow was enabled to bring an action for damages in two cases: (1) where she was deforced of her dower and (2) where suit was brought. Where dower (or curtesy) cannot be assigned, admeasured or set off without great prejudice to the owner, the court may order a sale of the real estate. The proceedings are the same as in a partition action where actual partition cannot be had. The sale may be free of dower or curtesy and compensation made for the value thereof.¹ A sale in partition proceedings, nor are the partition proceedings, for the benefit of the dowress; she cannot institute them, they are instituted only by one or more tenants in common.2 By statute, all estate, right of interest in or to dower or curtesy may be sold. And where an inchoate right is ordered sold, the selling master may pay to the tenant in fee and his wife the portion that would otherwise be invested, upon the receipt of his full release and discharge. It has been determined that where the land is in possession of a tenant by dower, then neither partition nor sale can be decreed without such tenant's consent."

$50. Tenant in Dower or Curtesy.

A tenant in Dower (or curtesy), is a life tenant and is subject to the same principles of law as a life tenant. As such, she cannot commit or suffer waste and must keep the premises in repair.¹ A writ may be brought in Chancery against such a tenant, and if convicted, "... shall lose the thing or place wasted and shall be liable in thrice the damages assessed against him by a jury.' » 2 $51. Release of Dower (or Curtesy).

The wife bars her dower not by any words of release in the deed, but by executing and acknowledging according to law, the conveyance of her husband which will be effective to pass title. Such acknowledgement must be duly certified by the officer taking the same.¹ No express words $50. 'Haulenbeck v. Cronkright, 23 E 407, affirmed 25 E 513. Porch v. Fries, 18 E 204. See Gaines v.

Since dower is a right in property it cannot be released or conveyed by parol. A plea that she had accepted and received a sum of money in satisfaction of dower was bad. 

The most a law court can do is to permit such payment to be given in evidence in mitigation of damages. Keeler v. 111

of release in the deed is necessary.2 A separate release by a feme covert will not extinguish her right of dower because her husband did not join. The deed must be a joint deed and a separate deed of the wife will not pass her title. The wife must acknowledge the deed according to the form prescribed by statute and where she signs, but the signature was never acknowledged, dower is not barred."

As set forth in $41, fraud on the part of the husband, does not deprive the wife of inchoate right of dower." But where the wife joins in the deed, where the husband was insolvent, to prefer one of the creditors, she is not entitled to dower.10 Or where by her voluntary conduct she creates an estoppel to her husband's false representation that he was single.¹¹

$52. Release or Bar by Operation of Law. 

Statutory Provision Not Affecting. 

The statute making lands liable for debts for one year after decease also contains the following: "... nothing herein contained shall affect right of dower or curtesy in said real estate." To the same affect the statute pertaining to descent of real estate² says that nothing shall be construed or taken to bar or injure the rights or estate of dower or curtesy. The same act giving a married person a fee simple in lands bought by the other during coverture, where no other disposition was made and no children left,³ was not repealed by the 1927 Amendment."

Divorce. 

It is essential to the estate that marriage shall subsist at the death of the spouse. One cannot have dower (or curtesy), who is not the wife (or husband) of the one in whose land it is claimed at the time of death. A valid

Bar by Adultery. Adultery by a married person who goes away, continues to live with his or her paramour, is a bar to dower or curtesy. The same rule applies where a wife after being ravished consents to the ravisher. Of course, if there is a voluntary reconciliation, then jointure, dower and curtesy are restored.10 Adultery by the other spouse, after, does not excuse the first one's acts so as to entitle that one to dower or curtesy." 11

$53. Election-Bar by Devise.

Statutory. 

"If a husband or wife makes a valid devise of real estate to his or her spouse, for life or otherwise, without expressing whether or not it is intended to be in lieu or bar of dower or curtesy, the surviving spouse shall not be entitled to dower or curtesy in any real estate devised by the will, unless, within six months after probate of the will, he or she files with the surrogate of the county wherein he or she resides or wherein the real estate so devised to him or her is situate, a written refusal to receive the same in satisfaction and bar of dower or curtesy in other real estate, by which refusal he or she shall be deemed to have renounced the devise in bar of dower or curtesy. 991 

This statute, being in derogation of the common law 

853. R. S. 3:39-1. The purpose of the statute is to ascertain and settle the rights of the parties so that one could not claim as devisee and dowress, and to compel the surviving spouse to elect between the will and her legal rights, in those cases where lands were devised to her. This prevents the surviving spouse from enjoying both-Stark v. Hinton, 1 E 216; and to make certain of the title of the other devisees after the lapse of six months -Moore v. Moore, 84 E 39, 92 A 948 affirmed 85 E 150, 95 A 983. Moore v. Gordon, 85 E 150, 95 A 983, (E&A).

The question to be determined is whether any lands or real estate were devised to her for life or otherwise. The value of the devise whether greater or less than the value of her dower is no consideration, since she has the election to take her dower." A devise of a chattel interest under the statute is no bar to dower as this section only applies to devise of real estate. CHAPTER  

$58. Distinction Between Sale and Exchange.

The word "exchange" has a special, technical meaning, where used in connection with real property, that cannot be expressed by any other word or in any other way.' It is a mutual grant of equal interests in land, the one in consideration of the other. The exchange must be of equal interests of estates (value is immaterial), and the deed must provide for re-entry upon ouster or eviction."

A contract of bargain and sale is a contract or bargain by the owner of the land in consideration of money or its equivalent to sell land to another person. The fact that 1 $58. At Common Law the words excambium or exchange might have been used. Technical exchanges of land are practically obsolete in modern conveyancing, mutual deeds of bargain and sale being substituted therefore. Haber v. Goldberg, 92 L 367, 105 A 874, (E&A). 

2 A fee simple for a fee simple, a lease for 20 years, for a lease for 20 years and the like-2 Bl Com 323; Haber v. Goldberg, Supra; Fidelity Union Trust Co. v. Prudent 

Inv. Corp., 129 E 255, 19 A 2nd 224; Smith v. Colonial 

Woodworking Co., 110 E 418, 160 A 351. 

Haber v. Goldberg, Supra. In a bargain and sale deed, by the Statute of Uses, a use arises in favor of the vendee to whom seizin is transferred. 131

part of the consideration, or all of it, is land does not of itself make the transfer a technical exchange." In an exchange only one conveyance is required and to this law annexes an actual, and not implied, warranty, with a condition of re-entry. If title to either tract of land turns out to be bad, and the party or his assigns should afterward be evicted, the one evicted can recover back the tract which was given in exchange. When property is subject to mortgage encumbrances, an exchange also differs from a sale in that no equitable duty is imposed upon the grantee to pay off the mortgage encumbrance in indemnity of the grantor as would be the case in a sale by which the mortgage encumbrance is treated as part of the consideration money. 

$59. Distinction Between Contract of Sale and 

Option.

Whether a writing is construed as an absolute contract for sale and purchase, or only an option to purchase, depends not on any particular words or phrases, but on the intention of the parties. Such intention is to be derived from the instrument itself by a consideration of all its parts, and when that is doubtful, from the circumstances attending it. A contract for sale and purchase creates a 

* Haber v. Goldberg, Supra. 

5 Haber v. Goldberg, Supra. 

® Smith v. Colonial Woodworking Co., Supra. 

1 $59. Sooy v. Henkelman, 104 L 540, 142 A 17, (E&A); Padula v. Komishane, 104 E 409, 146 A 200. 132

mutual obligation on the one party to sell and on the other party to purchase. An option merely gives the right to purchase, within a limited time, without imposing any obligation to purchase.2 In Equity, no estate in land passes under an option, the optionee only acquires an inchoate right to the land, pending the option period.³ 

Where a contract calls for payments to be made, and the purchaser does not agree to purchase, to make payments, or bind himself in any other way than the forfeiture of the payments already made, an option and not a contract to purchase is effected.* In an option to purchase contained in a lease, the payment of the rent is the consideration," but

An option must contain the full terms of the agreement and must satisfy the Statute of Frauds in all respects." 

An option may also arise where a vendor wrongfully sold the land, and later recaptures the title and is then able to fulfill his contract. In such case the option to take the price or the land is absolute in the vendee."

Acceptance of Option 

An option is a continuing offer and is irrevocable for the time of its continuance. In order to convert an option into a binding contract upon the one to buy and the other to cell, the optionee has the duty of communicating his mental assent and acceptance of the terms of the offer." $60. Contract of Sale-In General. 

A contract of sale and purchase has been defined above.¹ All five essentials, a distinct communication, offer and acceptance; a legal consideration; capacity of parties; for a lawful object or purpose; and in the form required by law apply to these contracts as to all others.2 The fact that a vendor does not hold absolute title to the land does not invalidate a contract of sale.³ In Equity, a paper defective as a deed for the conveyance of real estate will be treated as a contract to convey, and be enforced, if a substantial and valuable consideration has passed between the parties. Only those questions peculiarly affecting real estate transactions between vendor and purchaser will be considered in this chapter. $61. Statute of Frauds-In General.

A signature by an agent for an undisclosed principal is not sufficient since such contract does not contain the names of the buyer and seller.* Since an auctioneer, as between vendor and purchaser, is the agent of both parties, therefore the signature of the defendant's name made by the auctioneer at the time of sale is sufficient signing within the statute." Where the written contract is made by an agent, in order to be binding on his principal, it must purport on its face to be made by the principal, and must be executed in his name, and not in the name of the agent. 

Consequently it cannot be shown by parol that an alleged agent, who is stated in the contract to be a party thereto and who signed as principal, in fact signed as agent of another. $63. Difference Between Memorandum and Agreement

But where the writing is complete and definite in all its terms the question whether an informal arrangement is intended by the parties to form a contract in and of itself, or as only a step in the negotiations leading up to a binding contract, is not always easy of solution. Although the memorandum does not in terms state an agreement to sell, it complies with the Statute of Frauds, if from the consideration of the whole memorandum, it may be gathered that it was the intention of the one party to convey, and the other party to purchase. The rule may be stated that although the parties have agreed on all the terms of the contract, if it appears that the parties meant to have the terms reduced to writing and signed before the bargain shall be considered as complete, neither party will be bound until that is done, so long as the contract remains without any acts done under it on either side.³ $63. 2

It will be sufficient to satisfy the Statute of Frauds if its terms can be gathered from two or more detached papers, if the signed memorandum contains such reference to the other papers as to make the latter part of the former. The connection between the signed and unsigned papers cannot be made by parol evidence that they were actually intended by the parties to be read together, of facts and circumstances from which such intention may be inferred. The connection between them must appear by internal evidence derived from the signed memorandum. Description of Property. 

In an agreement or memorandum for the sale of land the sole purpose of the description is to identify the subject matter with certainty. The land to be conveyed must be definitely ascertained so that it can be distinguished from other land. Reasonableness in the description is all that is required, and it is not material in what way the requisite certainty is ascertained." A legitimate test of the sufficiency of identity under the Statute of Frauds is whether the description would pass title to the lands if devised by a will. Therefore, the mere omission of the State, the township, or county, does not make the description indefinite or vague." Reference to old surveys, streams, lots by number, or corner on public streets affords quite a certain means of indentity as would description by metes and bounds. The uncertainty of description is held to be avoided where the contract itself gives the right of choice * Force v. Dutcher, 18 E 401; Claphan v. Barber, 65

Welt, 98 E 602, 131 A 585. * Price v. McKay, 53 E 588, 32 A 130. 'Price v. McKay, Supra. n this case the description of a house was by street and number, the name of the city was not necessary.

Terms of Credit 

Generally terms of credit cannot be supplied by intendment and should be set forth.28 The question to be ascertained from the agreement is whether or not it was the intention of the parties that there was to be an extension of credit and that the time of payment should be postponed.29 Contemplation of payment in cash may be inferred from the agreement, therefor, an omission of the time for payment in the memorandum is not wanting for certainty. Where the price is named, without stating any terms, cash is meant, payable on demand or within a reasonable time.30 The omission of a due date of a mortgage does not render a contract uncertain and the parties will be presumed to have intended the mortgage to be a demand mortgage.31 The omission of the rate of interest raises 28

$66. Agency.

The authority of an agent to make an executory contract for the sale of land need not be in writing and may be created by parol.¹ Such authority to sign can be established by clear and decisive proof that it had been expressly conferred, or by proof of circumstances from which its grant may be reasonably inferred.² But a purchaser, buying of an agent, must, at his peril, satisfy himself to the extent of the agent's authority. Giving an agent the power to sell, by parol, does not confer on the agent the authority $66. 185 of the Statute of Frauds exacts a written contract not a written power to the agent. Force v. 

Dutcher, 18 E 401; Milne v. Kleb, 44 E 378; Long v.

30 A 1063 (E&A); Monohan v. McElligott 136 E 306, 41 A 2nd 399..

242, 30 A 619. 

2 Lindley v. Keim, Supra; Monohan v. McElligott, Supra.

to make or sign a written contract. Irrespective of the authority of the agent, if the principal cannot be compelled to convey on his own promise to sell or convey he cannot be compelled on such promise made by his agent.5 Where an agent signs without antecedent authority the principal may ratify the acts of the agent, as in other contracts. By Statute 5 of the Conveyance Act' the power given to an agent to convey land must be signed and acknowledged and must also be recorded.
