$68. Oral Contracts and Part Performance 

The equitable doctrine that part performance of a parol agreement takes it out of the Statute of Frauds rests on the principle that to plead the statute in the particular instance would work a fraud.' The acts of part performance must be of such a nature that the plaintiff cannot be placed in his original position, or adequately compensated in damages.² 

The following three requisites must concur before which courts of equity may enforce such contracts: 

1. The parol agreement relied on must be certain and definite in its terms and its existence clearly proved. 

2. The acts proved in part performance must refer to, result from, or be made in pursuance of the agreement proved.* stock, 135 A 65, 4 M 953, affirmed 103 L 701, 137 A 917; 

Walls v. Christos, 104 L 81, 139 A 3. 1 $68. Johnson v. Hubbel, 10 E 332, 66 Am Dec 773; 

Gilbert v. Trustees, 12 E 180; Green v. Richards, 23 E 32; 

Tunison v. Bradford, 49 E 210, 22 A 1073. 

Brown v. Brown, 32 E 650 (E&A); Green v. Richards, Supra. a Cole v. Potts, 10 E 67; Wallace v. Brown, 10 E 308; 

Brown v. Brown, Supra; Richman v. Richman, 117 E 226, 175 A 179; Cooper v. Colson, 66 E 328, 58 A 337, 105 Am 

St Rep 660, 1 Ann Cas 997. *It must be something done with the actual or constructive assent of the vendor-Camden & Amboy R. R. 158

3. The agreement must have been so far executed that a refusal of full execution would operate as a fraud upon the party, and place him in a situation which does not lie in compensation."

If these three things do not concur then one can only seek compensation in a court of law on a quantum meruit.® 

These exceptions from the requirements of Statute of Frauds have been held by the courts not to be carried farther than compelled to by former decisions."

Possession

A parol agreement to convey lands is not a license to enter." In order that possession of itself, constitute a part performance, it must be of such a nature and under such circumstances that it must be connected with the contract.12 If possession is taken forcibly and against the protest of the vendor,13 or if possession is referrable to some other cause,¹* then it will not be a part performance. Possession and part payment will take the case out of the Statute of Frauds,15 and so will possession and the making of improvements.' 16

In Consideration of Services

Generally, special acts of personal service may be adequately compensated for by a recovery of damages.¹ In order to take such agreement out of the Statute of Frauds it must appear that the services were in some respects of unique or exceptional character. It must be obvious that the parties did not only intend that such services should be measured by ordinary pecuniary standards, but that they were of such peculiar character that it is impossible to establish their value by any such standard.¹8

Assumption of Mortgage 5 Where the mortgage is part of the consideration money there is an assumption of the mortgage. An agreement to assume a mortgage is collateral and independent of the transfer of title; it does not affect the operation of the deed and may be proved by extrinsic evidence. A parol agreement of the grantee that he will assume the mortgage indebtedness upon the land conveyed, as part of the consideration of the conveyance, is valid and enforceable." 

Such a promise is not within the Statute of Frauds as a contract for the sale of lands, nor is it a promise to answer for the debt, default or miscarriage of another." 8

sioned by the neglect or default of the seller in carrying out the contract.³ Possession

There are two kinds of possession, actual, which gives the purchaser the dwelling, or the land for immediate use. 

The other is fictitious and emblematic, under the Statute of Uses. This Statute says that the bargainee of the use shall be deemed to be in possession. The purpose is to give validity to the deed as an instrument of conveyance. Ordinarily taking possession is a leave, license, permission and authority. Actual possession requires an explicit covenant.* 

Where the contract does not provide when possession should be delivered the purchaser is not entitled to possession prior to the time for the consummation of the contract." The right of possession and rents goes with the legal title so that all rents falling due before title passes belong to the vendor. But where the vendee goes into possession under the contract, he is entitled to the profits of the land

Based on the theory of equitable conversion, a person in possession of property under an agreement for a conveyance suffers the loss if the property is destroyed. If the purchaser has paid the purchase money, or paid part, and is personally liable for the remainder, the property is his property, in fact, and loss or destruction falls upon him as much as if the formal, legal title was in him.8 

Such a contract and transfer of possession effects a complete transition of the equitable and beneficial ownership from the vendor to the vendee and is such a change 'Brown v. Norcross, 59 E 427, 45 A 605; Hall v.

Martin v. State Ins. Co., 44 L 485, 43 Am Rep 397; Ordway v. Chase, 57 E 478, 42 A 149.

in interest, title and possession sufficient to avoid a policy of insurance as against the insurance company. $74. Time of Performance

The fixing of a time for performance is not an essential element of the written contract, because in its absence a reasonable time will be implied.¹ Where the time is fixed, without more, it is regarded as a formality only.² Generally, time is not treated in Equity as of the essence of the contract. Relief will be granted if that party has not been grossly negligent, and comes within a reasonable time, although he has not complied with the strict terms of the contract.³ 

Time of Essence

Time, in a contract for the sale of property may be made material and essential (1) by express stipulation of $74. Lean v. Leeds, 92 E 455, 114 A 402, (E&A);

The burden of proof is on the parties claiming that time of the essence was implied, and such intent must be shown clearly, unequivocally and unmistakably. A new agreement merely extending the time for performance is not to be regarded as making time of the essence and is only considered as a postponement.10

$75. Specific Performance-In General.

A bill for specific performance is addressed to the extraordinary jurisdiction of a Court of Equity to be exercised according to its sound and reasonable discretion.¹

The remedy is not granted unless the right thereto is clearly and conclusively established by the party seeking it.² The court is chiefly concerned with the equities of the parties and the relief rests upon what, in view of all the circumstances, the court ought to do according to the justice of the case. The remedy is governed by strictly equitable con- tion whether the other party can be put in default before the new time fixed for performance. The case of Barba v. Gunsberg, 3 M 714, 129 A 747, holds that consent to extend the time cannot be arbitrarily rescinded without giving the other party a reasonable time within which to comply with the terms of the agreement. 1 $75.¹ Stoutenburgh v. Tompkins, 9 E 332; Van Doren v. Robinson, 16 E 256; Crane v. DeCamp, 21 E 414; 

Meidling v. Trefz, 48 E 638, 23 A 824, (E&A); Blake v. 

Flatley, 44 E 228, 10 A 158, 6 AS 886 (E&A); Pratt v. 

Lyons 51 E 308, 27 A 934; Hemhauser v. Hemhauser, 110 

E 77, 158 A 762; New York Joint Stock Land Bank of

Ridgewood Park Estates, 120 E 520, 186 A 724; Mayo v.

Ridgewood Park Estates Supra, rescission was denied and the court refused to grant specific performance to the adverse party because other considerations rendered it inequitable, citing Blake v. Flatley. The court must determine which are the objectionable circumstances which will control its jurisdiction in such cases, within the established rules of equity, though none of these rules are of absolute obligation and authority in all cases.

Hardship or unfairness that is not inherent in the contract, and is brought about by subsequent events, does not relieve one of performing his contract. Such subsequent events or changed circumstances should have been in contemplation of the parties as possible contingencies when entering upon the agreement. He who seeks performance must show himself ready, desirous, prompt and eager to perform the contract on his part; and he must 6 Supra. In judging the fairness of a contract the court will look not merely to the agreement itself, but at the relationship of the parties and all the surrounding circumstances Crane v. DeCamp, Supra; Hemhauser v. Hemhauser, Supra. 

This principle should not be confused where there is a direct attack on the instrument itself to set aside a contract on the ground of inadequacy of price. The equitable principle is that the court will not interfere with bargains on the ground of inequality alone, unless it is so extreme as to shock the conscience, and so amount to evidence of fraud-Rodman v. Zilley, Supra; Worth v. Watts, Supra, and cases cited.

Building to be Erected

Generally the Court of Chancery will not enforce a contract for building or construction.22 The fact that the contract is one for the sale of real estate with a building to be erected by the vendor does not change the rule. The vendee is limited to an action at law for damages for the failure to carry out the contract.23 But where the vendor sues for specific performance, the law reads into the contract that the building shall be erected in a reasonably good and workmanlike manner and, when completed, shall be reasonably fit for the intended purpose. Such failure on the part of the vendor will deny him specific perform- 20

If the purchaser is in possession, delay in asserting such failure is not a good defence to such an action brought by his vendor.25 $76. Laches.

Since the complainant in a specific performance action must show himself ready, desirous, prompt and eager to perform, any unreasonable delay on his part in doing those acts which are to be done will justify and require a denial of relief.2 The rule of laches applies even when time is not, either expressly or impliedly, of the essence of a contract and rests upon the plain ground that granting specific performance would be inequitable and unjust.³ Suit must be instituted, not only within a reasonable time, but before any material change affecting the interests of the parties has taken place. But where, by the contract of sale, the vendee expressly assumes the risks of the title, or agrees to take such title as the vendor is able to give, the contract is enforceable, except where no title whatever can be given.' If there is doubt as to the validity of the title, the bill may be held until the question of title is properly determined in a court of law."

Where the vendor is required by the court to deliver a deed with a warranty against encumbrances it is his duty to remove an encumbrance even if there is doubt that the lien did not constitute any great impairment of the validity of the title-Dunwoody v. Goldfarb, 107 E 270, 152

The purchaser could not hold possession because of defective title and equity refused specific performance and under such inequitable conditions left the vendor to his remedy at law under the contract. 

Such stipulation that there be no objection to the title must be clear, plain and explicit-Bowen v. Vickers, Supra.

Where time is not of the essence, the law fixes the final decree as to when the title must be marketable. In the absence of some special equity the court has no discretion to extend the time for perfecting the title beyond this.' Where there are outstanding liens the purchase money can be used to pay them and the requirements of a clear title will be satisfied.8

The sure test is for the court to decide whether or not the title is marketable, not whether or not it is good.

Whether or not specific performance will be decreed 204

$78. Mutuality.

Generally, the want of mutuality of remedy and obligation is a bar to specific performance.¹ The absence of a reciprocal equitable remedy under the contract renders it unenforceable by the complainant whether he be the vendor, or the vendee. It follows, therefore, that where the vendor is a stranger to the title which he has agreed to convey, specific performance by such vendor of a contract to convey real estate will not be decreed. E 512, 139 A 484, affirmed 103 E 371, 143 A 917. 1 ce .... $78.

Coast Finance, 129 E 161, 18 A 2nd 268; all cases cited this section.

Where the title is acquired and can be conveyed at the time for the performance of the agreement specific performance will not be refused on the ground of lack of mutuality, but where the vendor is a stranger to the title, which he agreed to convey, the vendee has a right to repudiate the contract. $79. Mistake.

Because the equitable remedy of specific performance is governed by equitable considerations, it may be withheld where there has been a mutual or unilateral mistake and the writing does not give expression to the common intention.¹

To the general rule that ignorance of the law furnishes no excuse there is the exception, in equity, that whenever the mistake of the law is mutual, and the result according to law would be different from what they mutually intended, Equity will grant relief, provided that the party jeopardized thereby can be relieved without substantial injustice to the other side.2 A mistake or misapprehension of the effect of the contract on the part of the defendant could be a defence in a suit for specific performance, although it would not affect a suit at law, or be ground for 1 §79. The court will not compel performance of a contract which the defendant did not intend to make or which he would not have entered into and had its true effect been understood-Williams v. M. E. Blatt Co., 95 

E 326, 123 A 262; Union Fur Shop v. Max Melger, Inc., 133 E 416, 29 A 2nd 873 affirming 132 E 380, 28 A 2nd 

202, (E&A). 

2 Freichenecht v. Meyer, 39 E 551, reversing 38 E 315; 

Bowen v. Pursel, 109 E 67, 156 A 649, affirming 100 E 

319, 134 A 665, (E&A). In Hummer v. Buerk, 90 E 97, 106 A 141, there was a supposed flaw in the title and a subsequent agreement was entered into. On discovery of the mistake specific performance was granted of the original agreement. 211

setting it aside in Equity. But a mistake as to the legal effect of the agreement will not relieve the defendant, unless he was let into it by fraud or misrepresentation by the complainant.*

If a term intended to be inserted in a contract is omitted by mistake, such omission may be set up as a defence in a suit for specific performance." Where there is a mistake in fact it may be stated that, since the remedy by specific performance is discretionary, the rule holds that the question is not, what the court must do, but what, in view of all the circumstances of the case in judgment, should it do to further justice. To be taken into equitable consideration in applying this rule are questions of negligence, injustice and inconvenience to the other party,' misrepresentation and fraud, the fault of one of the par- 6 

3 Hawralty v. Warren, 18 E 124, 90 Am Dec 613. *Hawralty v. Warren, Supra; Harrington v. Heder, 109 E 528, 158 A 496. "But the utmost effect the proof of the mistake can have is to require the court to consider the contract as if the omitted words had been inserted in their proper places -McCormick v. Stephany, 57 E 257, 41 A 846. 

Anglescy v. Colgan, 44 E 203, 14 A 627, 9 A 105, (E&A); And see C 5 on Notice. "Freichenecht v. Meyer, Supra; McKelway v. Armour, 10 E 115, 64 Am Dec 445.

$80. Effect of Clause for Liquidated Damage. 

The mere presence of a stipulation for liquidated damages for breach of contract does not make the contract an alternative one.¹ The primary object of the contract is deemed to be performance and not non-performance.2 

Such a provision is intended merely to give security for performance, and not to give an option to perform or pay damages. Equity in such case will disregard the provisions ' In McKelway v. Armour, Supra-the court granted relief to the complainant although he was more at fault than his opponent. Isbill v. Duffy, 110 E 429, 160 A 326, (E&A).

§81. Effect of Rescission. 

It is a well settled principle of law that one cannot seek inconsistent remedies. When a purchaser institutes suit to recover the deposit money, he irrevocably commits 

4 Avon by the Sea, Land Imp. Co. v. Thompson, 60

93 E 505, 116 A 926-the circumstances were held to indicate an alternative contract. Such a clause should appear with reasonable clearness and certainty-Rittenhouse v. 

Swiecicki, Supra; Nolan v. Kirchner, Supra.

In re Tatnall, Supra, "without any further liability of any kind whatsoever" on the part of the vendee and the further clause if the vendor cannot convey "this agreement shall be at an end, and no further or other action shall be taken thereon," it was manifest that the agreement was not subject to specific performance. 5 

5 Storch v. Tepperman, 99 E 48, 131 A 626. Such a clause is for the purchaser's benefit-Epstein v. Mundweiler, 97 E 375, 127 A 547, (E&A); See Section 81 infra. • Poloha v. Ruman, 135 E 297, 38 A 2nd 287. 214

himself to a repudiation of all obligations under the contract and will not thereafter be entitled to specific performance.¹ Likewise, when a vendor elects to avoid the contract by ejectment on the basis of a forfeiture clause, this is an election to rescind the contract of sale and he thereby destroys his right to make a conveyance of the land.2

Since this rule is based on the doctrine of election of remedy it does not apply where the remedies are not inconsistent, such as seeking reformation of the contract. 

Nor does a discontinued vendee's action to recover damages for the non-performance of the contract, or a discontinued vendor's action to recover the balance of the contract price, bar specific performance.³ In such cases the original action is either an affirmance and not a repudiation of the contract, or a concurrent and consistent remedy.

In General 

The remedy of specific performance being discretionary and granted or refused, according to the justice of the case, Equity will not interfere when the complainant has practiced any fraud or been guilty of misrepresentation in any material particular.' The one rule of universal application whether a contract will be refused specific performance, or enforced, with or without abatement, is to determine under the circumstances of the particular case, what is equitable and just between the parties.2 

Such right to compel specific performance with abatement is based on equitable principles. Chancery may decree a conveyance of the vendor's actual interest, and allow to the vendee a pecuniary compensation, or abatement from the price proportioned to the amount and value of the defect in title or deficiency in the subject matter.³ 

13 Pierson v. Lum, 25 E 390. $83. Miller v. Chetwood, 2 E 199. Fraud and misrepresentation, See Section 91 infra. 

2 Van Blarcom v. Hopkins, 63 E 466, 52 A 147; Brisbane v. Sullivan, 86 E 411, 99 A 197 reversing 83 E 282, 93 

A 705. 3 

³ McCormick v. Stephany, 61 E 208, 48 A 25; Van 

Blarcom v. Hopkins, Supra; Brisbane v. Sullivan, 86 E 411, 219

4 This jurisdiction of chancery is a power to be exercised sparingly and with care and caution. Partial performance with compensation will only be decreed when the amount of loss for a partial failure of title can be ascertained with a reasonable degree of certainty, and is not a mere matter of speculation. The court will not, under the guise of a bill for specific performance, entertain a suit for compensation or damages which might be recovered at law. Neither will specific performance be decreed with compensation for partial compliance where the bill does not ask for it, or aver that complainant consents to accept it.' $84. Vendor's Right to Specific Performance and Abatement. 

Whether the vendor's failure to comply with the terms of the contract is a defect in title, or a deficiency in the suit of land he may obtain specific performance with

compensation if the defect is not material. The test in such case is not the vendee's willingness, but whether the vendee is getting substantially what he contracted for.' In such cases the defect may be merely formal or a literal failure to comply with the exact terms of the contract. But where such defect is material or substantial, or if it concerns an important or essential matter, and the purchaser will not get substantially what he contracted for, the contract will not be enforced unless the purchaser so consents. But even where the defect may be slight, and the contract contains covenants providing against such defects, notwithstanding such slight defect, the covenants are not to be disregarded. The reason being that it is the policy of the law to permit parties to make their own contracts and to disregard such a clause would be to make a new contract for the parties. When the court finds that the vendee has waived the defect the vendor may have specific performance with compensation.* Chancery has the general power to decree that the purchase money be applied to pay outstanding encumbrances and liens where they may be re- 3 §84. ¹Griggs v. Where the vendee entered into the contract in ignorance of a defect he may compel conveyance of whatever the vendor has, with a proportionate abatement from the price, even though the defect is considerable.' The rule prevails whether the deficiency relates either to the quanti-

If, however, the vendee knew of the defect when he made the contract, and entered the contract with his eyes open, he has no claim to an abatement of the price, although he may, if he sees fit, accept the land with its defects and pay the full price. In such case he may compel conveyance of whatever the vendor is able to convey, without any abatement. Such rule, however, is subject to equitable principles depending on the relationship of the parties, etc., and, of course, will not apply contrary to the express terms of the agreement.®

Where relief is granted on an executed contract the mistake must be sufficient to induce the court to believe, if the truth had been known at the time, the parties would not have contracted." $87. §89. Inability of Vendor to Convey. 

Where the vendor is unable to convey, because of that which is inherent in the subject matter itself, specific performance will not be decreed against him because such a decree would be nugatory.¹ "Holmes v Thorpe, 5 E 415. 

10 Saldutti v Flynn, 72 E 157, 65 A 246. 11 

¹¹ Stone v Stanley, Supra; Cohen v Rosenblum, Supra. 

12 Saldutti v Flynn, Supra; McVoy v Bauman, 93 E 

360, 117 A 717 affirmed 93 E 638 117 A 725. 

13 Thomas v Flanagen, 99 E 198, 132 A 305. 

14 See C2 on Dower and Curtesy. §89. ¹Hopper v Hopper, 16 E 147. 231

Whenever the inability arises before the bill is filed, and such inability is known to the complainant, the bill cannot be sustained for damages only. But where the complainant files his bill in good faith, believing that specific performance could be obtained, not knowing of the defendant's inability to perform; or where such inability is caused by the act of the party after suit has commenced, equity will retain the suit and award the complainant a compensation in damages. Equity takes jurisdiction in order to prevent a multiplicity of suits. The same rules would apply where the vendee by a parol contract has so far and in such manner performed on his part, that he cannot be restored to his former position, and if he has no adequate remedy at law, equity will give relief by compensation in money.* $90. Fraud and Misrepresentation.

This general subject is to be considered in relation to the question of notice and independent investigation contained in a subsequent chapter (5) on notice.

In cases of fraud, chancery possesses a general and concurrent jurisdiction with courts of law.' Where a vendee has been injured by reason of misrepresentation of his ven-

What is fraud must be determined on the peculiar facts of each individual case.' It is not every untruthful statement that will invalidate a contract. In the absence of trust and confidence, mere expressions of opinion, estimate or judgment, even if false, do not constitute fraud. But false statements of a fact does constitute actionable fraud.

Generally, an actionable misrepresentation consists in a false statement respecting a fact material to the contract and which is influential in producing it.10 "Kunz v. Barnegat Pines Realty Co. 109 E 115, 156 

A 417, (E&A). • Wuesthoff v Seymour, 22 E 66; Bowker v Cunningham, Supra.

Of Title 

The rule that Equity may grant relief although the false representations were innocently made does not apply where a conveyance is made containing full covenants and warranty of title. In such case the representation that title is perfect, if made in good faith, and the vendor believed that such representations were true, it is deemed that the vendee relied on the covenants of title, and not on the representations.30

Duty of Disclosure 

As set forth in the preceding section, in the absence of any fiduciary or trust relationship, the doctrine of caveat emptor applies between vendor and vendee and the mere circumstance of unequal business experience or mental powers does not defeat this principle.31 But there may arise, from the nature of the dealings, and the circumstances of the transaction, of the parties towards each other, an implied relationship of trust and confidence in that particular transaction which Equity regards as essentially fiduciary.32 In such case the nature of the transaction is not the test and each case must depend on its own circumstances.33 Where such relationship is found to exist silence may be fraudulent. A party under a duty to disclose is guilty of fraud when he conceals a material fact within his own knowledge.³4

E 366, part of the consideration was the transfer of stock of a corporation and in the negotiation the defendant declined to express an opinion of its value. The right to rescind was given when the stock was proved to be worthless.

Where the question of title is involved, in rescission, the rules of law are not the same as in specific performance; and the defect must be substantial. In actions at law the implied or express agreement requires a title good at law, upon proofs under the rules of evidence. $94. Rescission-Waiver.

Once the innocent party elects to rescind or not to rescind he must abide by his decision, and such election is irrevocable.¹ Where fraud or defect is involved, the innocent party may waive the fraud or defect in which case he may proceed under the original contract.2

A waiver is "a voluntary relinquishment of a known right" and should be distinguished from "estoppel." An estoppel is based on equitable principles and consists of "a preclusion which in law prohibits a party from alleging or denying a fact in consequence of his own previous act, averment or denial." Where there is a waiver the party so relinquishing his known right cannot reclaim it without ment was from 1-2 to 5/8" and rescission granted. In Blum 

Bldg. Co. v Guaring, 6 M 245, 140 A 881 a garage encroached fourteen feet on another property. The porch of a building encroached on the public street and the eaves overhung the highway in White Way Corp. v Heinle, 6 M 472, 142 A 667. 

Where the agreement does not contain any such clause the vendor may have specific performance and the purchaser given an abatement of the purchase price. See also

the consent of his adversary. But the bar of an estoppel may be lifted by the party against whom it was invoked by giving the proper notice. To constitute a waiver there must be a clear, unequivocal and decisive act of the party showing such a purpose or act amounting to an estoppel. 

So a purchaser's consent to take the premises subject to a defect of title may be held a waiver if supported by an agreement found on valuable consideration. 

Evidence of waiver of the right to rescind include payments of purchase money, after knowledge; continued dealing with the property purchased as if the contract was still subsisting and binding; retaining possession and selling part of land. $97. Liquidated Damages.

A sum of money specifically set forth in the contract as "liquidated damages," in case either party shall fail to perform, is a stipulation of the measure of indemnity that one is bound to pay and the other is bound to accept in full satisfaction and compensation for the breach of the agreement. Such stipulation is mutually binding when damages sustained by a breach are uncertain, and are not susceptible of accurate ascertainment by any method of legal computation.¹ In an action for damages at law there is no distinction between agreements for liquidated damages and an alternative contract.² The intention of the parties, whether such sum is a penalty or a provision for liquidated damages, is to be determined from the nature of the agreement and the surrounding circumstances. Although the word penalty is used the contract may be construed as one for stipulated and ascertained damages. Likewise the words "li- 1 3 $97. Cheddick v Marsh, 21 L 463; Monmouth Park

Ass'n. v Wallis Iron Works, 55 L 132, 26 A 140, 19 LRA 

456, 39 Am St. Rep. 626! Baerenklau v Peerless Realty 

Co., 80 E 26, 83 A 375. er "... and indemnity for the injury is incapable of being ascertained with precision by the rules of evidence by which compensation is ascertained in the trial of causes"- 

Whitfield v. Levy, 35 L 149, (E&A). 

2 Although the word penalty prima facie excludes the notion of stipulated damages-Whitfield v. Levy, Supra. * In such case the construction that it is a penalty must be overcome by very clear evidence to the contrary, de- 267

quidated damages" may be rejected if there was no intention that the entire sum should be paid absolutely on the non-performance of any of the stipulations. In such case the clause may be construed as a penalty only.* 

Where there is doubt such provision in the contract will be construed, by the courts, as a penalty." If the clause applies to many different provisions of the agreement, of various degrees of importance, it will be construed as a penalty, unless the agreement specifies the particular provisions to which the liquidated damages are to be confined. 

To be considered liquidated it must be susceptible of being so regarded as to all the provisions to which it extends, and, if some may be ascertained by legal computation, will be held to be a penalty."

When the damages are to be sustained by the breach of a single stipulation, and they are uncertain in amount and not readily susceptible of proof under the rules of evirived from other parts of the agreement-Cheddick v Marsh, Supra; Whitfield v Levy, Supra; Baerenklau v 

Peerless Realty Co., Supra. *The law favors mere indemnity from the rule of actual compensation. Monmouth Park Ass'n. v. Wallis Iron 

Works, Supra; Bonhard v Gindin, 142 A 52, (E&A);

Wallis Iron Works, Supra; Bonhard v Gindin, Supra; 

Baerenklau v Peerless Realty Co., Supra. 'Whitfield v Levy, Supra. 268

dence, and such sum is not disproportionate to the amount of loss, such sum may be recovered as liquidated damages. 

But where the sum named is wholly disproportionate to the actual damages sustained the courts will construe such provision as a mere penalty to secure performance and not for liquidated damages."

Where construed to be a penalty the measure of damages is the actual damages whether they are greater or less than the penalty set forth.10

Use and Occupation An action for use and occupation requires the relationship of landlord and tenant. Such relationship does not exist between the vendor and vendee of real estate where the purchaser goes into possession prior to the delivery of the deed. The failure or refusal of the vendee to perform the contract does not ipso facto convert the relationship to that of landlord and tenant. In order to create such relationship a reservation of rent and a fixed term is required." 

If this is not present, a stipulation in the agreement that the vendor may take proceedings to remove the purchaser by summary proceedings is of no effect since such jurisdiction cannot be conferred by agreement. The vendee in possession is not a tenant for the purpose of sustaining an 5 The reason is that an implied contract cannot arise where there is an express contract between the parties relating to the same subject matter. $100. Vendor's Lien.

The basis of the equity to a vendor's lien is "that a person having got the estate of another, shall not as between them, keep it and not pay the consideration.” No special agreement for such purpose is necessary and the right exists unless there has been a special agreement extinguishing it. The very essence of the lien is that it follows the land and is to be made out of the land, and if that remedy is not available, the vendor's lien cannot exist.³

A vendor's lien does not arise to secure the performance of any act or claim for unliquidated damage. There- 1 $100. It is the right to come to a court of Equity and ask it to appropriate the property for the payment of the debt-Boorum v Tucker, 51 E 135, 26 A 456 affirmed 

52 E 587, 33 A 50.

This lien is an invisible trust-Van Doren v Todd, 3 

E 397. A constructive trust that the purchaser shall be a trustee of the land for the vendor, until the purchase money is paid-Graves v Coutant, 31 E 763, (E&A).

3 Bliss v Linden Cemetery Ass'n., 90 E 404, 107 A $94, affirmed 91 E 329, 109 A 500-In this case the lands were conveyed to cemetery associations which are dedicated to a public use. A trust to a public use is repugnant to a use of the vendor. 274

fore, where the consideration is other land or personal property, with no monetary price fixed, there can be no vendor's lien.' The lien exists as against persons holding under the vendor with knowledge—that is, notice of the indebtedness, and that such indebtedness arose upon the purchase of the property."

The lien prevails against even a purchaser or mortgagee for a valuable consideration from his grantee with notice of the unpaid purchase money. It also prevails as against one who takes title by act or operation of law from such grantee without notice.' It is not necessary that a subsequent mortgagee or purchaser should have had notice of the existence of the lien; it is enough if he knew that the purchase money was unpaid. as a waiver or discharge of the vendor's lien, if the purchase money has not, in fact, been paid.¹³ 

The taking of an unsecured note, bond, or covenant from the vendee does not of itself discharge or act as a waiver of the lien.¹ Such instruments are only the ordinary evidence of the debt and the land is liable, because the purchaser looks to the vendor alone for his money (and may be merely an extension of time for payment). Any act, which indicates that it was not the intention of the parties that the purchase money should continue a lien upon the land conveyed, is a waiver of such lien. Such lien may be waived or avoided by parol, or even orally, or by acts manifesting an intention to discharge the same, or by estoppel,15 or by conveyance to a public use.16 Thus, if the vendor takes any other than the personal security of the vendee for his money which would show that he does not look to the land as security, will be taken to be an implied waiver of the lien, and discharge the land from further liability. CHAPTER IV BROKERS 

$103. Preliminary.

This chapter will limit itself to the law pertaining to real estate brokerage in New Jersey, the licensing, and statutory regulations of the business, brokers' transactions as affected by the Statute of Frauds, a broker's rights against, and liability to, his principal, and rights as between brokers themselves. $104. Definition

By statute a real estate broker is defined as follows: 

"A person, firm or corporation who, for a fee, commission or other valuable consideration, lists for sale, sells, exchanges, buys or rents, or offers or attempts to negotiate a sale, exchange, purchase or rental of real estate or an interest therein or collects or offers or attempts to collect rent for the use of real estate or negotiates, or offers or attempts to negotiate a loan secured or to be secured by a mortgage or other encumbrance upon or transfer of real estate for others. 991

Commission has been defined as a percentage or allowance made to a factor or agent for transacting business for another. This definition should not be confused with $104. ¹R.S. 45:15-3. 

2 Malinowsky v. Lincoln Developing Co. 103 L 394, 

136 A 202, (E&A). 287

the requirements of Section 10 (Section 9 N. J. S. A.) of the Statute of Frauds. Salesmen 

A real estate salesman is "Any person who, for compensation, valuable consideration or commission, or other thing of value, is employed by a licensed real estate broker to sell or offer to sell, buy or offer to buy or negotiate the purchase, sale or exchange of real estate, or offers or attempts to negotiate a loan secured or to be secured by a mortgage or other encumbrance upon or transfer of real estate, or to lease or rent, or offer to lease or rent any real estate for others, or who is an employee, agent or associate of any person, firm, corporation or association owning lands or leasing lands, and offering such lands at either public or private sale as a developer where such development consists of more than twenty lots.' 994 

A salesman's remuneration, under the Broker's Act, is to be paid only by his employer, who must be a licensed broker. Otherwise, except for the licensing provision, the law pertaining to salesmen is the same as that pertaining to brokers. A salesman is prohibited from performing any of the acts covered by these regulations unless under authority of his broker's license. The revocation of a broker's

license automatically suspends the salesman's license, pending a change of employer and the issuance of a new license to the salesman.' When a salesman's employment is termin-ated, the broker must return the salesman's license to the commission. A broker, however, is not responsible for his salesman's violations unless he had guilty knowledge thereof.8 Autioneer

An auctioneer is one who, for pecuniary gain, conducts a public or private competitive sale of lands or any interest in lands.⁹ 

Distinguished from Agent

The relationship of a broker to his principal is that of principal and agent and is covered by the laws of agency. 

A real estate agent is a special agent.10 His agency is limited and comprehends only such authority as is expressly granted or arises by necessary implication." As an intermediary, he is in a sense, the agent of both parties." Where the real estate broker is employed to sell land, he has no authority as such agent, to enter into a binding contract for the sale

Particular Licenses 

All applications for licenses are made to the New Jersey Real Estate Commission and provisions are made for three classes of licenses: broker's license, salesman's license, and auctioneer's license.10 The auctioneer's license is not affected by any requirement of municipalities to regulate and license auctioneers and auction sales of real estate. 10 The applicant must be a citizen of the United States, of the age of 21 years or over, and if a corporation, the directors shall be citizens of the United States, of the age of 21 years or over. Every broker shall have first served an apprenticeship of one full year as a duly licensed salesman, and if a corporation, at least one of the officers must qualify as a broker."¹ Provisions are made for examination which the applicant must pass satisfactorily before his license can the note was, in part, such commission, as between the original parties, the maker had the right to raise the defense of partial failure of consideration.

be issued.12 The license must be displayed in the place of business,13 and the salesmen's licenses are to be kept in custody by the broker.¹4 

Suspension or Revocation of the Licenses 

Licenses may be suspended or revoked where 

1. The licensee is guilty of making false promises or substantial misrepresentations. 

2. Acting for more than one party to a transaction without the knowledge of all parties thereto. 

3. Flagrant and continued misrepresentation or making a false promise through agents, salesmen, advertising, or otherwise. 

4. Failure to account or pay over any monies belonging to others, coming into the licensee's possession. 

5. Any conduct and demonstration of unworthiness, incompetency, bad faith or dishonesty.15

Such violations do not relieve the license from civil liability or criminal prosecution.15 

Before any licenses can be suspended or revoked, the licensee is entitled to a hearing upon ten days' notice to answer charges duly made. The findings of fact made by the commission, in the absence of fraud, are conclusive, 12 R.S. 45:15-10.

Where the members act in performance of their public duty, they are not personally liable in a civil action for damages arising out of their acts, providing, what they do is done in good faith." The mere revocation of a license, without justification, was held insufficient in a suit for damages against the members of the commission; as private citizens, they could be held responsible only when coupled with physical interference of brokers' rights.' 18 

The Code of Ethics of the National Association of 

Real Estate Boards, adopted by most local boards, provides that the members submit claims against one another to arbitration.19 A real estate board decided that one member had no claim against another. He thereupon sued the owner and the board expelled him. This was held wrongful, inasmuch as the power of the board was limited to determination of controversies between members and did not extend the relation between members and outsiders.20

$106. Statute of Frauds

In order to constitute the relationship of agency between the broker and his client, a contract is necessary This contract is governed by the laws applicable to ordinary contracts and no matter what the form of the contract, there must be a meeting of the minds through offer and acceptance.' The mutual promises are in themselves a legal, adequate consideration. This rule holds, except as modified, by Section 10 ($9 of N. J. S. A.) of the Statute of Frauds, the first paragraph of which reads as follows: "Except as herein otherwise provided, no broker or real estate agent selling or exchanging real estate for on account of the owner shall be entitled to any commission for such sale or exchange, unless his authority therefor is in writing, signed by the owner or his authorized agent, or unless such authority is recognized in a writing or memorandum, signed by the owner or his authorized agent, either before or after such sale or exchange has been effected, and in either case, the rate of commission on the dollar or the amount of the commission shall have been stated therein." 1 $106. Grossman v. Calonia Land & Imp. Co., 103 

L 98, 134 A 740, (E&A). 

2 Cobb v. Bowes, 104 L 615, 141 A 810, (E&A);

This section was enacted for the protection of the landowner. It, however, was frequently used by landowners to 295

This statute declares a public policy, and in the absence of a written contract, there is no right to compensation for services.*

This section was held to apply to an attorney at law,5 to a salesman working on commission exclusively for the deprive a broker of commission which had been rightfully earned. In 1911, the statute was amended so as to make the owner liable if the authority of the broker was recognized in a memorandum signed by the owner or agent, whether or not the same was signed after the sale or exchange had been effected. In 1918, this act was again amended to give the broker the right, within five days after making of an oral agreement, to serve notice upon the owner in writing, etc. This is considered in Section

That public policy persists except as the legislature has changed it. This section applies where both real and personal property are involved in the contract, and the subject matter is entire and indivisible.10 Where part of it is void, this avoids the whole contract.10 $107. Sufficiency of Writing

A formal contract is not necessary between the owner and broker since the statute provides merely for a writing authorizing the sale, signed by the owner or his authorized agent.' Where the writing stated, "I shall be glad if you would sell the property, I will be glad if you would sell the property and get a commission," stating all the terms, that was held sufficient." The word "authority" as used in the statute means the power delegated by a principal to an agent and the use of that word in the writing is not necessary. But where the writing does not set forth the full terms of the agreement, it is not sufficient, such as, failure to provide the sale price,³ the mode of paying the price, except where the owner did not object to the method of

Rate of Commission 

This section requires that the rate of commission on the dollar or the amount of commission be stated, so the fixing of a sum certain is sufficient." Where the broker's compensation is the amount he might realize over and ´ above the sum named by the owner as his limit, this complies with the Statute of Frauds."

But where the contract provides for the regular brokerage commission, even though the rates were established and fixed, such a provision was barred by the Statute of Frauds. And where the contract merely allowed commission "at the legal rate of commission," the broker could not recover under this section."

It is not necessary that the terms be complete in one writing, but the Statute of Frauds will be satisfied if the signed memorandum contains such reference to the un- * Richard v. Spagna, 5 M 33, 135 A 468, affirmed 103 

L 711, 137 A 918.

Lustig v. Meirick, 82 L 498, 82 A 867, (E&A).

8 Smith v. Pazen, 5 M 832, 138 A 513.

The memorandum may be signed before or after the sale has been effected, so a clause in a written contract of sale, executed by the owner, is sufficient to recover commission even though this was never delivered to the purchaser." Where the broker was acknowledged in the vendor's written receipt of payment, stipulating the terms of the contract, this was held to comply with this section of the Statute of Frauds as against the contention it was a mere executory and rescindable agreement contemplating execution of a formal contract. 12 $108. The services must be rendered under an employment by his principal; services rendered as a mere volunteer gives no right to commission.* Whether a broker has earned his commission or not, depends on the terms of his brokerage contract. Where the owner sold to an officer of a certain corporation that was designated in the authorization, it was held that the broker was not entitled to a commission; this was so even where the sale was to the particular person with whom the broker was negotiating for the corporation. $111. When Commission is Earned.

The condition upon which the broker earns his commission is that he shall obtain a sale, and his right to recover compensation, in such cases, is dependent upon his obtaining a sale.¹ That of course, means a valid sale, an invalid sale is practically no sale.2

A real estate broker earns his commission when he secures a buyer, able and willing to conclude the transaction on the seller's terms either as originally propounded, or as settled by agreement between the seller and buyer.* 1 $111. Courter v. Lydecker, 71 L 511, 58 A 1093.

563, 12 A 2nd 881 holds that an unfulfilled condition in the contract is the same as no sale having been made, and since the broker's commission depends on consummation of the sale, no commission is earned.

Where the broker is the efficient or procuring cause of the sale, his right to recover is not dependent upon the knowledge of the vendor that the purchaser was procured by the activity of the broker,19 even where the broker had nonexclusive authority to sell.20 Neither is it dependent upon what parties formally entered into the written contract of sale, but wholly upon who constituted the "real" parties to the bargain.21 Where the broker is compelled by the agreement to inform the owner of negotiations with the prospective purchaser the contract governs." 16 22

Where the broker made only one attempt to get the parties together and several months later the parties entered into a different kind of agreement, on different terms, the broker was not the efficient or procuring cause and was not entitled to commission.23 But where there is some proof that there was no breaking off of negotiations, the question of abandonment is one for the jury. 24 In a case in which the prospect's attention was called to the property by the broker and there was a failure to agree on the price, the intending purchaser procured a secret agent to negotiate the purchase as an independent buyer, without the owner's knowledge. The owner being assured that there was no broker in the transaction made a concession in the price and the secret agent closed the contract. The Court of Errors and Appeals held that the broker was not entitled to commission against the owner even though the secret agent assigned the contract to the broker's purchaser.25

Time Limit

Where there is no time specified, the authority of the broker continues for a reasonable period depending on the circumstances of the case. If the contract contains a time limit within which he might consummate his agreement, the broker must perform within that time, unless the obligor has prevented performance by some willful or fraudulent act.37

Where there is a time limit to the broker's agency and the owner sold the property, after the broker's agency had terminated, to a purchaser with whom the broker had been in negotiation, did not entitle the broker to commissions.38 

But where the broker had secured the purchaser after the option had expired, it was held he was entitled to recover his commissions.39 In such case if the purchaser was procured within the time limit and a binding contract for sale and purchase was entered into, the broker was entitled. to 

37 Hinds v. Henry, 36 L 328. 

38 Loxley v. Studebaker, 75 L 599, 68 A 98, (E&A), held that the contractual obligation was terminated and the broker had failed to perform the condition upon which he was to be paid. But where the owner treats the contract as effective after the time has expired this is treated as a waiver. Richards v. Spagna, 135 A 468, 5 M 33 affirmed 

103 L 711, 137 A 918. 

39 Schwartz v. Weinstein, 104 L 368, 140 A 418 reversing 5 M 708, 138 A 113, (E&A). This decision is based on the wording of the contract and on the principle that the commission is earned when he secures a buyer on the seller's terms. 318

Time of Payment

9944 "on the date of closing title," "when title is passed," "to be paid at settlement, or where commission was to be paid upon the balance of the initial payment of the purchase price, merely fixes the time when the commission should be paid and does not create a contingency or condition precedent, unless directly or impliedly made so in the agreement. The general propositions hereinabove set forth apply to leasing arrangements as well,' thus, "rent" and "renting," as far as establishing the right of a broker to receive commissions, means the same as "sell" and "selling."The broker earns his commission when he procures a lessee able and willing to conclude the transaction upon the owner's terms, or where the landlord agrees to the terms and makes a binding contract.2 But where the tenant procured by the broker was a corporation without assets of its own and sponsored by no substantial interest, the broker was not entitled to commission.³

Exclusive Agency. 

A contract of employment does not give the broker an exclusive agency or an exclusive right unless it is so specified either expressly or by implication in the contract.¹ The average authorization is simply a naked revocable power. An exclusive agency must be distinguished 

* Stevens v. Oschwald Realty, Supra. 

5 Mau v. Vulcan Building & Loan Ass'n., 11 M 352, 165 A 888. * Tepperman v. Polster, 113 L 14, 171 A 834, (E&A). 'Vreeland v. Vetterlein, 33 L 247. 1 $113. Ettinger v. Loux, 96 L 522, 115 A 384, 20 

ALR 1265, 4 R.C.L. 259, (E&A). 321

2 from an exclusive right to sell the property. An exclusive agency merely precludes the owner from employing another broker and the owner does not thereby relinquish the right to sell the property himself, independent of the broker. Thus, where the owner makes the sale himself, he is not liable to the broker for commission.* However, if the broker had exclusive right to sell, he may recover commission for the same, from the owner, of any sale made by the principal within the time specified in the contract." And the owner selling himself before revocation of the contract, cannot deprive the broker of his commission." 

Moran v. Resnick, 131 A 912, 4 M 107, says that the commission on a broker's exclusive agency, was earned when the prospective buyer agreed unconditionally to purchase on the terms authorized. Richmond v. Miller Rubber 

Co. of New York, 6 M 787, 142 A 761. In Maxwell v.

The measure of damages for breach of a contract, "Is the amount which will compensate the plaintiff for the loss..... such as may fairly and reasonably be considered as arising naturally from the breach."14 This rule is similar to the measure of damages provided for in the Sale of Goods Act. So where a broker had an exclusive agency, he is limited to the loss sustained and the commission on the sale price was not the proper basis for estimating damages.¹4 

As Against Third Parties

As a general rule, an action on a contract cannot be maintained against a person who is not a party to it.15 As to brokers, under the Statute of Frauds, the right of action can be maintained on the basis of a contract between the vendor and purchaser.16

Where the broker has been employed by the owner, the purchaser cannot be held responsible for commission if the vendor defaults, unless, of course, there is an agreement with the purchaser. This rule is not changed in cases where the vendor conveys the very property which was sold and thereby divests himself of all its assets so that he cannot be 14 (E&A). 15

If he could purchase at a lower price either directly or indirectly he had a legal right to do so, and the loss of the prospective sale was an ordinary hazard of business dealings.18 The broker in such a situation could have protected himself by a special contract with the owner. 

A purchaser of real estate does not impliedly assume obligations that do not run with the land purchased."

Where the lease does not contain any express provision protecting the broker's rights to commissions, as against the successors in title or assignee of the lessor, an action for commission cannot be maintained against the purchaser of the property.20 The contention that the tenant's possession of the premises was notice of the broker's right to commission, subject to the rights of the tenant, does not hold since such possession is only notice of the possessor's rights and of nothing more. In Tort

A stranger, who intermeddles and defeats a broker's claim for commission is liable in tort.22 So where the officers of a corporation wrongfully induced it to ignore the broker, and the commission was later divided between them, was a good cause of action in tort." 22 23 

No tort action can be brought against one with whom the broker has a contractual relation because if he fails to prove the contract, he has no standing, and if he proves the contract, his recovery is for breach of the contract. A tort action is conditioned upon the wrongful, malicious and unjustifiable act of the third party. The purchase of the property by the vice-president of the corporation, with whom the broker had been negotiating, for use in a new business venture, did not show a conspiracy to defraud.25 24 v. $115. Rights as Between Brokers. 

Where there is no exclusive agency, it is the broker who in good faith and without fraud on his part "procures and delivers" (i.e. actually closes the sale to) the purchaser, that is entitled to the commission.¹ The principal acting in good faith and without fraud is only liable for the payment of commission to that agent.2 Thus where several brokers are openly employed, the broker through whose instrumentality the sale is completed, is entitled to commissions,³ unless there was collusion on the part of the vendor. In such a case the entire duty of the seller is performed by remaining neutral between the several brokers and he has the right to make the sale to a buyer produced by any one of them.* The seller is not called upon to decide between the agents as to which of them was the primary cause of the purchase. Two brokers co-operating in a real estate transaction, where they are to share commissions, or where one is to receive a specified sum, is not invalid.5 1 $115. A. W. Van Winkle & Co. v. Where brokers between them agree to share commissions, there is sufficient consideration if one procures for the other the listing of the property and where the tenant is procured, and the commission paid, such agreement will be enforced.

In such a venture the commissions are divided and the efforts of one to procure a purchaser will inure equally for the benefit of both. This is so though one does the major share of the work. Such an agreement contemplates an equal division of the commission in the absence of any other stipulation. In such a case where the broker asks another for a share of the commissions, an admission that the defendant has received commission is sufficient to prevent a non-suit.⁹

Negligence

The rule as to negligence has been stated by the Court of Errors and Appeals as follows: 

"The law is that men who undertake to do a service for another in a business or professional capacity, like real estate agents and the like, undertake to use the reasonable skill which is ordinarily possessed by their fellows in like service, nothing more and nothing less; if they fail in the performance of that duty, they are responsible to the injured person to the extent of the damages that either naturally flow from the wrongful act and are a direct consequence of it, or of those results, those consequences, which are in contemplation of the parties at the time the service is undertaken."80

In this case the real estate agent undertook to procure a certain designated lot and he negligently procured title to some other lot, without the knowledge of the purchaser. 

The real estate agent was held liable for ensuing damages, the result of the broker's negligence."

Liability to Third Parties

CHAPTER V  $117. Title-Definition 

,,1 In

Blackstone has defined title to lands as the "means whereby a man hath just possession of his property. its broadest sense title includes a leasehold estate as well as a freehold estate. It is applied to signify the right to land and real effects.2 Possession itself, is title. "technically speaking, of a low grade, it is true, but still it is title.”³ Such title is of itself, not an absolute title only a presumption of title. The right of possession and the right of property in lands are to be distinguished from actual possession.* From $117. 12 Bla Com 195; Halcombe v. Trenton White 

City Co., 80 E 122, 82 A 618, affirmed 82 E 364, 91 A 1069 

Campfield v. Johnson, 21 L 83. 2 

³ 2 Bl. Com 195; Hill v. Carter, 16 L 87. 

See Section 122, infra, on possession as notice, also 

C I, Section 20, note 7. * The right of possession may reside in one man while the actual possession is in another. This right of possession may be an apparent right of possession which may be defeated by proving a better. The right of possession may also be an actual right of possession, which will stand the test against all opponents. 2 B. Com 195; Hill v. Carter, Supra. 

The right of property is the "jus proprietatis" without either possession or the right of possession-2 Bl Com 337

the fact that possession is a form of title arises the rule of law that the one who has possession, may fold his arms in security, until his right is impeached.5

Our statute pertaining to recording and registry of documents affecting land-titles distinguishes between such titles as possession alone gives, and title by documents. 

Other legislative acts, such as dower and curtesy, and descent, confer title, independent of a present possession. 

Where possession, the right of possession and the right of property are joined a complete title is formed. Title may also be legal and equitable."

Distinctions between a bad title, a doubtful title, a good title and a marketable title have been considered in a previous chapter. For methods of acquiring title to real estate reference is made to a subsequent chapter."

§118. Inception of Title in New Jersey' 

The State of New Jersey, together with Virginia, Maryland, Pennsylvania and New York was originally granted by letters Patent from King James to Sir Thomas Gates and others. This Patent was repealed in 1623 and the lands reverted to the King.

In the year 1664, Charles II granted to his brother, the Duke of York, all the land, soil, with all the royalties and government of New Jersey. This grant was also by letters patent and in addition to making him proprietor of the soil gave him the right to exercise the authority of governing the province. In 1676, the Duke of York granted the same with like powers to Lord Berkeley and Sir George Carteret.

They in turn, in 1682, also by grant, conveyed what was then called East Jersey to the Earl of Berth, William Penn and others; and to that part which was called West Jersey to Edward Billings.

The proprietors were tenants in common of the soil. 

The original proprietors of East Jersey were twenty-four in number. By the terms of the grant they were invested with all the rights of the property and government which had been originally conferred on the Duke of York by letters patent of the King. In 1702, as a result of difficulties between these proprietors and the British authorities, the 

11 Bouviers. 1 $118. Arnold v. Mundy, 6 L 1; 10 Am Dec 356; 

Martin v. Den, Lessess of Waddell, 18 L 495; Ocean Beach 

Ass'n., v. Brinley, 34 E 438; Attorney General, ec rel. 

Ryerson v. Corp. of Newark, 6 E 201. 339

proprietors surrendered to Queen Anne of England, her heirs and successors, all the powers of the government, but retained the rights of the private property. The Revolutionary War changed the royal authority and rights so that they became vested in the people of New Jersey as the sovereign.2 The original proprietors severed their common estate by legal deeds of conveyances, either of a fractional part or of a certain number of acres. In order to convert the common stock which was severed into several rights, the proprietor of the grantee presented his title to the council and obtained an order for a warrant. This order was signed by the clerk and recorded. It authorized the surveyor-general or his deputy to survey that portion from any of the unappropriated lands. On the return of the survey, which recited the warrant and the deductions of the title with the map, the deputy surveyor certified it to the council, and, upon their approval, it was ordered to be recorded.* 

By an Act passed in 1787,5 after the survey was duly entered of record in the proper office, the proprietors and their successors were precluded and forever barred from any demand on those lands, any plea or deficiency of right or otherwise notwithstanding. Previously, in 1763, Indian titles has been declared void. 2

$119. Notice re: Title-In General 

Notice means knowledge or information. With reference to real estate titles it may be notice of record or not of record. The most common classification of notice is actual or constructive. The general doctrine, is that "whatever puts a party upon an inquiry, amounts in judgment of law to notice, provided the inquiry became a duty, as in the case of purchasers and creditors, and would lead to the knowledge of the requisite facts, by the exercise of ordinary diligence and understanding".' Fraud is the true doctrine in cases involving the doctrine of notice. 

Actual notice is such as may be proved by direct evidence. Constructive notice is just as effective as actual notice and may also be called implied notice. 

Constructive notice may be divided in four categories: 

1. Where the party charged had actual notice that the particular property was, in some way, charged, encumbered or otherwise affected, in conflict with his interest. The law in such case, binds him with the further information which constitutes actual notice.2 

Grath v. Norcross, 78 E 120, 79 A 85 affd, 82 E 367, 91 

A 1069. 1 $119. Lee v. Woodworth, 3 E 36; Smallwood v. 

Lewin, 15 E 60; Wahl v. Stoy, 72 E 607, 66 A 176; H. C. 

Tack Co. v. Ayers, 56 E 56, 38 A 194, 13 LRA (NS) 124; 

Boynton Real Estate Co. v. Woodbridge, 94 L 226, 109 A 

514, (E&A); Kennedy v. Island Development Co., 9 M 921, 156 A 233. 

2 Based on the maxim that such information was sufficient to put a reasonably prudent man, using ordinary caution, on inquiry-Hoy v. Bramhall, 19 E 563, reversing 19 E 74, 97 Am Dec 687, (E&A); Wahl v. Stoy, Su- 341

2. Where the party charged has designedly abstained from inquiry, for the very purpose of avoiding notice. 

3. That notice which arises from possession alone.* 

4. By statute, instruments duly acknowledged, or proved, and properly recorded or lodged are notice to all subsequent judgment creditors, purchasers and mortgagees of the contents thereof. This is equivalent in law to actual notice."

In addition to such notice as above set forth there are certain rights inherent in the property itself which arise from the marital status of the vendor or mortgagor (dower pra; Protection B & L v. Knowles, 54 E 519, 34 A 1083

$121. Independent Investigation

A person cannot claim lack of notice where he deliberately avoids it.' As an outgrowth of this rule where a purchaser makes independent investigation, of which he has been given the opportunity, the law conclusively presumes that knowledge reasonably available was obtained.2 Such rule is designed to prevent fraud in that it would be practically impossible to learn how much knowledge was obtained by such investigation. It is reasonable to assume that such investigation was made with care and completeness, and the purchaser cannot take advantage of his own carelessness or negligence. The rule has been applied to statements made by a vendor even though such statements were misrepresentations, since such investigations show that the statements were not relied on, but the purchaser preferred to investigate the matter for himself. But where a careful inquiry might have been fruitless of the facts of such misrepresentation the vendor is not excused.* 3 $122. Possession as Notice

The rule of constructive notice created by possession of real property grew up at a time prior to the development 1 $121. DeWitt v. Van Sickle, 29 E 209. See also Section 119, Note 3, supra.

If he neglects this duty he is charged with knowledge of every fact that he would have obtained had he performed it. To establish the fact of such notice, it is not necessary to show that the person to be charged knew of the possession by another since it is notice to all the world. Purpose of Recording

Acts regulating registry are Statutes of Notice. The title upon the record is to protect purchasers and encumbrances so as to prevent frauds and wrongful priorities. 

By the doctrine of caveat emptor one purchases land at his peril and the registry acts provides a means of looking to the title and the competency of the seller. 

The proprietary, utility and safety of conveyances of land titles depends on the recording acts for their security, 16 

Bingham v. Kirkland, Supra; Van Keuren v. The 

Central Railroad Co. of N. J., 38 L 165; Rankin v. Coar, Supra; Groton Savings Bank v. Batley, 31 E 273 affirming 

30 E 126; Hinners v. Banville, Supra.

Where an equitable owner occupies the land he must refrain from all acts calculated to produce a false impression as to the state of the title-Lathrop v. Groton Savings Bank, Supra. 18 

Bingham v. Kirkland, Supra; Rankin v. Coar, Supra. 

Rapine v. Harvey, 128 E 437, 16 A 2nd 822. 350

order and regularity. Not only do these acts provide for the safety of titles by the records, but they also provide a more easy and permanent mode of proof than was possible by the common law.

As notice they are not conclusive, but is such notice as would lead to proper inquiries, to enable each person to protect his interest. Despite the recording acts, the record may show a complete title, good on its face, but still be defective. There remains the possibilities of forgery; that the instrument may not have been properly witnessed or acknowledged, as alleged; a marriage may afterward be shown to have been illegal; or corporation officers may have lacked authority, etc. 

3124. Notices of Record-History of Recording 

As far back as 1676, the proprietors of West Jersey made provision for the recording of deeds, which, if left unrecorded would be void. In 1695, the legislature of West 

Jersey provided a penalty of 20 shillings on every person who should neglect or refuse to bring his deed to the proper clerk to be recorded within six months after its execution.

In East Jersey the constitution of 1683 provided for recording, and an act in 1698 required the deed to be recorded in the Register's office within six months after the deed was signed, sealed and delivered. Such deed was required to be acknowledged or proved and, when so recorded, was good $124. ¹Den v. Richman, 13 L 43; McGrath v. Norcross, 78 E 120, 79 A 85 affirmed 82 E 367, 91 A 1069; 

Glorieux v. Lighthipe, 88 L 199, 96 A 94, Ann Cas, 1917 

E 484; LaCombe v. Headley, 89 E 364, 104 A 711 affirmed 

91 E 63, 108 A 185. 351

and effectual notwithstanding any other conveyance although dated before such deed was registered. In 17131714 the clerks of several counties were required to keep books for the recording of deeds. Such records of deed, when duly acknowledged or proved, when produced by the proper clerks, were as valid in evidence as if the orig inal were produced and proved. This first provided for the recording in the county. This Act remained in force until 

1731 and subsequently was disallowed by the King. 

The 1743 Act provided for the acknowledgement and proof of deeds, for their recording, and provided that copies of the records of such, duly acknowledged and proved, to be good and sufficient evidence in any court of the Colony.

In 1784, the clerks of the several counties were required to provide and keep books to record such deeds and such copies were admitted in evidence. The recording was to be in the secretaries' office only. But this Act did not require such recording and no preference was given to a recorded over an unrecorded instrument.

It was not until an act passed on June 7, 1799, to take effect January 1, 1800, and a supplement thereto passed on November 26, 1801, that the 1784 Act was amended. These amendments provided that every deed of conveyance of lands should be void and of no effect against subsequent bona fide purchasers, or mortgagees for valuable considerution, not having notice thereof, unless acknowledged or proved, and certified and recorded, within six months after the signing, sealing and delivery. The deeds had to be recorded with the Clerk of the Court of Common Pleas. It was provided that such deeds or conveyances should be valid and operative as between the parties and their heirs. 

Such a deed if recorded within the six months was good as 352

against a subsequent deed to a bona fide purchaser without notice, if such subsequent purchaser recorded the deed before the prior deed.

In 1820 the time to record was cut down to fifteen days, and the act was again revised in 1874 making an unrecorded deed void as against subsequent purchasers if not acknowledged, proved, recorded or lodged within 15 days after its execution. Prior to the Act of 1883, the effect of the record of notice was determined by principles of equity and was limited to deeds in the chain of title to the person who was to be charged. But the act passed in 1883 made the record notice to all persons of the execution thereof omitting the fifteen day provision.2 The Act of 1883 was repealed in 1898, and a supplement passed in 1903, so that record, was notice only to subsequent judgment creditors, purchasers and mortgagees. But the record was not only notice of the execution of the deed, but also of the contents thereof. The purpose was to protect purchasers of land which had already been conveyed by an unrecorded deed by making such a deed of the same land void as to them.

This latter act is the basis for the present statutes under the Revised Statutes of 1937. $125. Requisites to Recording

This section should be read in conjunction with Section 136 infra, in which certain defects in recorded instruments are rectified or given full force and affect by validating acts. 2 Although, by construction, notice had been limited to the same class of persons prior to 1883. 353

Acknowledging Officer

Our Statutes provide that certain named officers may take acknowledgements within the state only, and that other officers may take acknowledgements only out of the state.18 For this reason the certificate of acknowledgement should state where the acknowledgement was taken, otherwise, the officer's authority may be questionable.19 The certificate should also state the title of the officer taking the acknowledgement so that it may be determined, from the face of the certificate, the authority to take such acknowledgement." 20 Within, the State of New Jersey, acknowledgements may be taken by any of the following officials of this state:21

The Chancellor; a justice of the Supreme Court; a judge of the court of common pleas; a master in chancery; an

In other states and territories acknowledgements may be taken22 by attorneys at law or masters in chancery of New Jersey; by the chief justice of associate justice of the 

United States Supreme Court; judges of the United States 

Circuit or District Court, judges or justices of the supreme or superior courts, and chancellors of the several states and territories; foreign commissioners of deeds appointed for the state of New Jersey in which he was acting, and under his seal; the mayor or chief magistrate of any municipality of that state, under the municipal seal; any officer of that state authorized to take acknowledgements or proofs, accompanied by the county clerk's certificate as to his authority. County judges may also take acknowledgements only in the county or district for which he holds office. Local officers are empowered to take acknowledgements and proofs only within the state, district or municipality for which they are appointed. The right of these officers to take acknowledgements and proofs must be shown by a certificate under seal of the state, or the court of record in which the acknowledgement was taken.

In foreign countries the following officers are authorized to take acknowledgments or proofs;23 any master in chancery of New Jersey; any public ambassador, minister, consul, vice consul, consular agent, charge d'affaires

Here also the local officers must have their authority certified to under the official seal of said court.

The officer taking an acknowledgment performs a ministerial act, and not a judicial act.24 A grantor cannot take his own acknowledgement, nor is it proper for the grantee to take the grantor's acknowledgement. But where the officer taking the acknowledgement and certifying it has a secret interest such interest will not avoid the acknowledgement. Where the interest does not appear on the face of the instrument it is valid and the registry constitutes notice.24

Conclusiveness of Certificate 

As between the parties, a certificate of acknowledgement made by a duly authorized officer is prima facie evidence that such instrument was executed by the grantor as his voluntary act and deed.25 Such certification being ex 

24 A judicial act ordinarily has reference to some controversy. In an acknowledgement there is nothing suggestive of controvery-Morrow v. Cole, 58 E 203, 42 A 673.

497; Colonial Bldg. Ass'n. v. Griffin, 85 E 455, 96 A 901; 

Walkowitz v. Walkowitz, 95 E 249, 122 A 835 (E&A). 361

ww parte, the party to be affected by the deed is allowed to question its validity, and the force and effect of the formal proof. But, to establish its untruth and overcome the strong presumption of its integrity, the proof must be clear, satisfactory and convincing. But it is prima facie evidence only as to those facts pertaining to the statutory authority of the acknowledging officer."

As to a bona fide purchaser for a valid consideration the officer's certificate is conclusive of the matters which are required to be certified in the certificate and is open to attack only in case of fraud.28 $126. The Search in General

Generally, as we have seen in a previous chapter,¹ the doctrine of caveat emptor applies to the purchase of real estate. In the absence of any special agreement, it is the duty of the purchaser or mortgagee to search the public records and to make a complete examination of such records, for such information as they may contain regarding the validity of the title of such real estate. Period of Search 7

Judgment searches should cover a period of twenty years before title is acquired as against every one in the chain of title, and for twenty years before he conveys the property to another grantee. Sufficient additional time should be added and investigated to see that no execution was issued before the Statute of Limitations took effect.:7a 

Where a judgment is found in the prior chain of title, executions should be searched to forestall the possibility of a sale having been made of the property between the date of the entering of the judgment and a subsequent sale by the sheriff on execution.
