profession, however, is to go back for a period of 60 years to preclude any questions that may arise where cotenancies existed and in cases of disability. $127. Abstracts and Examiners

After a contract of sale has been entered into and before final title is approved the examination of the record title is made by a searcher who abstracts the title for the reader. The abstract of title should contain an abstract of every record upon which title depends and which would affect such title and should be put in suitable form for examination. Methods of reading titles vary and may be by diagram or analysis; or one may first complete the chain of title and then ascertain the encumbrances. 

An examiner gives legal opinion of title which is based on the abstract. He determines in whom title vests, whether such title is free from defects, which would render the title unmarketable; whether there are encumbrances; and whether the provisions of the contract of sale have been complied with. The title examiner issues a certificate of title or certifies it to a title company. The title examiner's certificate should be as to the exact status of the title as he found it. A title company, in turn, issues an insurance contract in which the company agrees to indemnify the purchaser or mortgagee, the assured, for a loss to the extent stipulated by the terms of the policy. 

Spottswoode v. Morris & Essex Railroad Co., 61 L 322, 40 

A 505 affirmed 63 L 667, 44 A. 1100; O'Brien v. Bilow, Supra. 367

Liability of Attorney 

An attorney employed to examine title to real property is required to make a painstaking examination of the records and to report on the facts relating to the title. He must exercise reasonable skill and care, and his contract is that he use the reasonable knowledge and skill which lawyers of ordinary ability and skill possess and exercise.¹ His duties to his client have been likened to those of a physician and patient. Unless there is an express agreement the lawyer is not an insurer, nor a guarantor, nor is he answerable for an error of judgment.³ But he is responsible to his client for negligence in the performance of his duties.* Such negligence would be "a failure to exercise ordinary care and skill in discovering in the record and reports on deeds, mortgages, judgments, etc. and that affect the title in respect to which he is employed". The measure of damages where the attorney passed as clear a title encumbered with liens is the amount ne- $127. ¹ Jacobsen v. Peterson, 91 L 404, 103 A 983 affirmed 92 L 631, 105 A 894; McCullogh v. It is a well settled principle of law that the Statute of Limitations does not run against the state or municipality except where expressly provided for by Statute," or by necessary implication that such was the contemplation of the legislature.13 $130. Recording Acts-Extent of Notice-In General 

The recording acts are statutes of notice and apply only to those instruments for which express provision is made by Statute. An instrument is void and of no effect only in cases declared by Statute, other instruments not within these provisions retain their natural, original and independent effect. Where there is no Statutory authorization for recording there is no notice.¹ Where the record shows an instrument as valid there is no burden of inquiry imposed to go behind the record unless other facts are shown to raise a presumption of invalidity. This applies notwithstanding that there is notice of only a nominal con-

$131. Defective and Improperly

Recorded Instruments 

Defectively executed instuments are void and do not act as constructive notice except as validated by Statute.¹ 

Thus, an unsigned instrument, an unacknowledged or one improperly acknowledged,³ in the wrong form, at common law, one lacking a seal," were void. Wrongful recording likewise is not constructive notice and is equivalent to no record at all: such as recording in the wrong books, or in a different county than that in which the lands are situate.' 1 $131. If the record fails to disclose or specify the property granted a purchaser will not be bound, but ambiguity, or error on the face of the record, ordinarily will be sufficient to put purchasers on inquiry. But a defective registry cannot qualify the effect of actual notice.¹º The Statute¹¹ does not make the index an essential part of the record but only requires the instrument to be duly recorded or lodged for record. $132. Bona Fideness

The protection of the Statute¹ extend as to bona fide purchasers and mortgagees for value.2 Those who are not bona fide are left in the same situation as before. The presumption is that one is a bona fide purchaser for value and the burden of proof is on the one who alleges to contrary, unless there is some circumstances of which the purchaser is bound to take notice. In defense to an action all the facts of a bona fide purchaser must be alleged clearly and unequivocally, and that the purchase was without notice, and must be distinctly proved.* The essence of the doctrine of a bona fide purchaser is that he part with something of value on the strength of the apparent ownership of land, founded upon an original $132. 

R.S. 46:22-1.

He must have altered his position irretrievably in actual reliance upon the apparent title of the other party to a particular piece of property. The giving of a security and executing a bond or other obligation for payment is not sufficient." The payment of a previous debt is not within the protection of this Statute, but an absolute payment and discharge is sufficient to make the creditor a bona fide purchaser. The distinguishing feature is whether it was taken as security or in satisfaction

Another exception, and not within the registry acts, is where two mortgages are given at the same time to the same person. There is no right of preference, since they are concurrent liens, and priority of record does not give a right of preference in payment to one.16 Although such mortgages are recorded at different times they still remain concurrent liens. Where two persons hold such mortgages they may agree between themselves as to the order of priority, but such agreement, being a latent or secret equity, is without force against a bona fide assignee.¹7

§134. Names 

In searching the chain of title the question often arises as to the identity of the particular person. From the citations of the reported cases, subject to the doctrine of actual notice, certain principles may be deduced as to how far similarity of names would affect the doctrine of constructive notice. The first principle, of course, is that the recording system should be made effective and the obliga- 19 

Lovejoy v. Lovejoy, 31 E 55; Williams v. Gilbert, 37 E 84; Bogert v. Lydecker, 45 L 314; Riverside B & L 

Ass'n., 93 E 508, 131 A 78; West Hudson Co. Trust Co. v. Wichner, 121 E 157, 187 A 579.

Such priority of lien gained by the execution and levy cannot be affected by any execution subsequently issued, nor by any mode in which the lands may be sold- 

Clement v. Kaighn, 15 E 47; Lippincott v. Smith, 69 E 

787, 64 A 141, (E&A). 20 

Jenkinson v. New York Finance Co., Supra. See also Section 130 Note 6, Supra. 

21 Sections 133 and 134. 393

tion of the searcher is limited to that similarity of names which would suggest inquiry. The test should be: Is such similarity sufficient to put a reasonably prudent man, using ordinary caution, on inquiry?

In tracing titles to land there is an inference of fact that identity of name indicates an identity of person creating a prima facie presumption.' The suffix "Sr." or "Jr." is no part of a name.²

Thus the search is limited to the real names of those in the chain of title and not fictitious names. The name of a party known as "Barbi," also known as "Barboe", does not charge one with notice that title may have been in the name of Borbely.*

By the common law, a legal name consists of only one given name, and of one surname, or family name." Thus it is sufficient if one is known as "Margaret Kinney" as of "Margaret N. By virtue of the provisions of the evidence act' the proper certificate of acknowledgment or proof² constitutes prima facie evidence of the execution of such instrument.

This section entitles such instrument to be received in evidence in any court or proceeding in this state. 3 Also by Statute, the record and certified copy of any instrument pertaining to real estate, provision for which has been made for recording, which has stood on record for a period of six years, shall be received in evidence in every court, and be as effectual as if the original deed or instrument had been produced and proved. This latter "Yucker v. Morris, Supra. 

8 Englesse v. Hyde, 111 L 1, 166 A 468, (E&A). "Englesse v. Hyde, Supra. $135. 

R.S. 2:98-20. 

2 See $125 supra. 3 R.S. 46:16-1. 

395

Statute requires, in addition, corroboration or explanatory proof of corresponding enjoyment or its equivalent. It only applies as evidence after the six year period. Where such instrument, however, is recorded after the expiration of ten years from the date of its acknowledgment, such certified copy is not admissable in evidence. 

Affidavit of Title, etc. 

The right to record is given also to all instruments "of or affecting the title to real estate in this state." No express provision is made for the recording of affidavits of title, although it is possible to attach such affidavit to the deed. Such affidavits, of themselves, do not affect title to real estate. Therefore, they cannot be admitted in evidence to prove the facts contained, not even of presumptive proof. The mere production of such an affidavit is not sufficient to clear a cloud on the title. Provisions should be made for the recording of such affidavits in the same fashion as deeds, etc., are recorded. They will then become of public record for the purpose of locating predecessors of title, to show marital relationship, etc., and be always available to everyone.

The same may be said of certified copies of the resolutions of boards of directors of corporations authorizing particular transfers of real estate. 5 

R.S. 46:16-8. This Statute requires the original instrument to be kept by the recording officer. • Tzeses v. Green, 105 E 12, 146 A 593. 396

$136. Validating Acts

Fom time to time the legislature of the State of New Jersey approves certain acts pertaining to instruments of record validating certain informalities or irregularities in such instruments or their execution. Such legislative acts have the effect of making title good, valid and sufficient notwithstanding such defects. Among such Statutes pertaining to real estate are:

A deed or conveyance on record for one year or more is sufficient to convey the estate of the grantor notwithstanding any informality, imperfection, uncertainty or defect in its acknowledgment or proof.¹ If on record for 

5 years or more any instrument pertaining to real estate is good and sufficient, in law or Equity, notwithstanding any errors or imperfections in its acknowledgment or proof.2 An acknowledgment taken by a grantee is good if such instrument is on record for 5 years or more. Where the acknowledging officer failed to qualify, or where his term. of office had expired, such affidavits, acknowledgments and proofs have been validated.*

The lack of a seal in an instrument executed by an individual does not void the instrument, if the attestion or testimonium clause, or the acknowledgment or proof, $136. 

R.S. Val: 4-8.2.

3 R.S. Val: 11.18. * R.S. Val: 11.19. 397

recites that the same was signed and delivered. A similar statute applies to deeds of real estate by corporations made prior to June 14, 1952. All such instruments where the seal was omitted are validated if on record for at least 5 years, if good and valid in all other respects." 

If a corporation has not been completed and is no longer exercising the power of a corporation any deed made by such corporation before April 1, 1936, is validated provided the certificate of incorporation was filed in the county, notwithstanding that it was never filed in the state; and provided that the deed was recorded withishe calendar year the certificate of incorporation was recorded in the county." 10 Errors or defects in conveyances made by corporations now defunct, by or to religious groups or corporations, clubs and lodges¹¹ are validated under the provisions of the particular statutes pertaining to each.

Merger is the conclusion of law upon the union of two estates and is the extinguishment of the lesser. Merger is the effect while union is the cause. 2 At law the rule is inflexible and intention is the governing principle. But, in equity, merger is not favored and is never allowed unless for special reasons to promote the intention of the parties. Such intention may be actual or presumed. It is the intention of the person in whom the interests are united. Where the lesser estate is of no use, to such person, to have the charge on his own estate kept up, it will be held to be extinguished, unless something shall be done by him to have it kept up. where it is perfectly indifferent to such person whether the charge should or should not subsist. It will take place, not only according to the actual and just intention of the party, but it must also serve the purposes of justice." If the purpose is not innocent, or if injurious to someone, the doctrine of merger will not operate despite the intention.

Cases interpreting the doctrine of merger vary considerably and are not always reconcilable. Since we are primarily concerned with legal title, and, subject to the rules pertaining to trusts and concerning equitable principles, merger takes place where an equitable and legal estate unite in the same person. In such case the equitable estate will merge in the legal. This rule is based on the maxim that a man cannot be trustee for himself." An uncancelled mortgage of record is not a cloud on the title where the record discloses that the mortgagee acquired the fee from the mortgagor. Likewise a defect in title may be cured by a conveyance to one in possession." Clos v. Boppe, Supra; Tennenberger v. Sozio, 101

sum sufficient to pay the monthly proportion of taxes, assessments, and insurance as hereinafter set forth in clauses b and c. Said payments are to be made until the whole of the said principal sum shall have been paid. The first of the payments herein set forth shall be made on the 1st day of Interest is to be paid on all deferred payments, computed on the balance remaining due from time to time, from the date hereof until paid at the rate of per cent per annum, payable on the 1st day of each month. b. To advance herewith a quarterly payment of taxes which shall become due on said premises, and to pay all taxes as hereinabove set forth and all future assessments that may be levied, assessed or become due upon the said premises before any penalty or interest would accrue thereon.

C. To insure and keep insured in at least the sum of dollars ($............) against loss or damages by fire, lightning or tornado, during the life of this. agreement, the buildings now or hereafter erected upon the said premises in a duly licensed company, with loss, if any, payable to the vendor as his interest may appear, and to deposit the policy or policies with the vendor. The first yearly premium for said insurance is to be paid herewith. 

2. That any contemplated repairs, alterations and improvements on the premises aforesaid, or any part thereof, shall be first submitted to the vendor for his approval and consent, which shall be in writing. A copy of each and every such contract and of the plans and specifications for such repairs and improvements shall and will be promptly delivered to and may be retained by the vendor. 

3. That the purchaser shall not and will not transfer or assign this agreement or any of his interest therein, nor 414

lease or sublet said premises or any part thereof, without the previous written consent of the vendor. Any such assignment, transfer or lease, without such previous written consent, shall not vest in the transferee, lessee, or assignee any right, title or interest herein or hereunder or in said premises, but shall render this contract null and void, at the election of the vendor. 

4. That no right, title or interest, legal or equitable, in the premises aforesaid, or any part thereof, shall vest in the purchaser until the delivery of the deed aforesaid by the vendor, or until the full payment of the purchase price at the times and in the manner herein provided. 

5. Provided, however, and this agreement is upon the express condition that, if default shall be made in the payment of the said several sums or any of them, or any part thereof, or in the peformance or observance of any of the covenants herein contained on the part of the purchaser, then the whole of the said principal sum, with accrued interest thereon, shall at the election of the vendor become immediately due and payable. Upon default the vendor shall have the right to terminate this agreement by giving to the purchaser days' written notice of such termination, time being of the essence of this agreement. In the event of such termination all payments made by the purchaser hereunder shall be retained by the vendor as liquidated damages for the breach of this agreement by the purchaser, and the vendor shall have the right to reenter and take possession of the said premises with all the buildings and improvements then thereon. It being agreed that until such default the purchaser shall have possession of the said premises. 

6. In the event this contract or agreement shall be declared null and void by the vendor on account of any 415

default, breach or violation by the purchaser in any of the provisions hereof, the same shall become and be null and void and be so conclusively determined by the filing by the party of the first part, of a written declaration of forfeiture or release hereof, in the Recorder's Office of. County. 

7. It is further expressly agreed between the parties hereto that the remedy of forfeiture herein given to the vendor shall not be exclusive of any other remedy. The vendor shall, in case of default or breach, or for any other reason herein contained, have every other remedy given by this agreement and by law or equity, and shall have the right to maintain and prosecute any and every such remedy, contemporaneously or otherwise, with the exercise of the right of forfeiture, or any other right herein given.

IN REM FORECLOSURE CONDEMNATION FORMS PUBLISHER'S PREFACE 

This pocket part or supplement brings our four volumes of Real Property Law in N. J. up-to-date to July of 1960. It is very important to the lawyer, title searcher and reader, that this supplement be consulted to find changes in the law brought about by the new cases of the past couple of years. 

The test of a good lawyer is his knowledge of a subject and the changes therein. This supplement renders a real service to the average lawyer by saving him time and increasing his ingenuity. Brevity and quality are always in demand.

To satisfy many requests, we have created a NEW COM- BINED INDEX for all four volumes that will expedite research enormously.

The Bench and Bar owe a debt of gratitude to both author and publisher for the production of this work. 

H. S. ROSENBERG, President 

Associated Lawyers Publishing Company

§2. Definition of Property. 

Inland bodies of water are divided into two legal classifications, public and private. New Jersey has long used the test of ebb and flow of the tide to distinguish the two. A lake is usually a private lake. The owners of the bed in an inland lake usually have the sôle rights to the recreational uses of the waters. With tide water the title of the grantee goes to the middle thread of the stream. With a natural pond, the grant extends to the middle of the stream in its natural state.¹ 

Boundaries may be proved by locations of monuments, and any other kind of evidence which establishes the boundaries. 

If a deed is clear but the description became ambiguous because of a missing iron pipe which was a monument in the second course, the problem was not one of construction of deed but of location of the lands described.2 §32. Joint Tenancies and Tenancies in Common Distinguished.

Where a husband and wife take property in their two names but it is revealed later that they are not legally married because the husband's prior divorce was invalid, they take the property as tenant in common.¹ 

§33. Joint Tenancies and Estates by the Entirety. 

Because of the common law doctrine that husband and wife are one their joint ownership of real property creates an estate by the entirety. A divorce will create a tenancy in common. There is no estate by the entirety in personal property. 

If the property is sold on condemnation, or foreclosure, or money received from insurance, then the estate by the entirety continued in the money, but it does not if the prop-

This case overrules Zanzonico vs. Zanzonico, 24 N.J. Misc.

§34. As between Husband and Wife. 

If title is obtained in the name of persons as husband and wife and it is later revealed that they are not legally married, they become tenants in common.¹ Money received as damages to property which is an estate by the entirety becomes money in an estate in common.² 

§38. 

Definition of Dower.

A surviving spouse has two interests in the realty of which the deceased spouse dies seized: the right of quarantine, N.J.S. 3A:35-4-the temporary right to remain in and use the mansion house—and a life estate in one-half of all of the realty. The widow has the right to occupy the homestead or rent it to others until dower or curtesy is assigned. At common law the right of quarantine was enjoyed only by the widow and was usually limited to 40 days and its purpose was not only to provide the widow with a dwelling place, but also to compel the heirs to make an assignment of dower. 

Now under the statute both the husband and wife are entitled to quarantine.¹ 

§39. Section of Curtesy. 

An inviolate right of curtesy arises out of the marriage relation and upon the death of the wife it becomes consum-

f the er att Torsn d and ried mes mate in the husband. Its extent is determined by the statute in effect when she becomes seized of the property and in this instance it is 1 full and equal half part of all real estate whereof his wife was seized of an estate of inheritance. 

There is no difference between unassigned dower and unassigned curtesy. The rule barring dower upon the death of the widow prior to assignment of dower is controlling as to rights both of curtesy and dower accruing since the adoption of the statute now found in N.J. S. 3A:35-2. A widower could ask that his curtesy interest be transmuted into an interest in the proceeds of sale, either by way of allowance of a gross sum or by investment of a proper share for life. 

If he fails to do either, no court can order a payment of a gross sum in lieu of curtesy without the authorization of a statute. Curtesy may be waived or barred on the theory of estoppel, if the husband's act or conduct is inconsistent with his claim.¹ $45. Assignment.

Under the statute now existing there is no basis for difference in treatment between unassigned dower and unassigned curtesy. The statute requires both kinds of rights to be "enforced, admeasured and determined in the same manner." N.J.S. 3A-35-2. The widow or widower has the right to occupy the homestead or rent it to others until dower or curtesy is assigned. A widower could compel an assignment of curtesy. A widower, who is a parent and a fiduciary of the heirs of the deceased spouse, is under an obligation to have the curtesy interest allotted at once or have someone else appointed as guardian ad litem for the minors so that he may have his curtesy interest promptly assigned or evaluated. It is a breach of the widower's fiduciary obligation to defer application for assignment of curtesy with the result of a claim on behalf of his estate to include the right of a surviving spouse in quarantine to occupy the realty free of the obligation of payment of taxes, interest on mortgage, or maintenance repairs, as permitted by the cases. When a 

§39. 1 In re. Flasch 51 N.J.Super. 1. 143 A. 2nd. 208. 

7

widower has curtesy assigned to him he would become a life tenant, charged, pro tanto, with the payment of taxes, interest on mortgage and repairs.¹ 

§58. Contract of Sale Distinguished. 

Where an agreement was made that no sale is to be made without offering the sale to the person mentioned in the contract, a grant of an easement to another party was not a sale because the owner of the servient estate still owns the fee and has all the rights and benefits of ownership consistent with the enjoyment of the easement. But the easement is subject to the agreement to sell.¹ 

$59. Between Contract of Sale and Option. 

There is a material difference between a conventional option and a right of preemption. An ordinary option gives the optionee the power to compel the owner of the property to sell it at a stipulated price, whether he be willing to part with ownership or not. On the other hand, a privilege of first refusal or preemption does not give the preemptioner the power to compel the owner to sell; it merely requires the owner, when and if he decides to sell, to offer the property first to the person entitled to the preemption, at the stipulated price, and the preemptioner may then elect whether he will buy. The preemptioner's right to a conveyance depends upon the grantor's either notifying him that he desires to sell or offering or contracting to sell the premises to a third party without first giving the preemptioner the opportunity to buy at the stated price.¹ 

Where there is an option to purchase within time, and time for closing under the contract was not of the essence or made so by the parties or their action, and suit was instituted for specific performance forthwith, specific performance will be granted where the purchaser acted in good faith §45. ¹ In re: Flasch 51 N.J.Super. 1, 143 A. 2nd, 208. 

$58.59. 1 Wellmore Bldrs. Inc. vs. Wannier 49 N.J.Super. 456 140 A. 

2nd, 422.

POCKET PART-SUPPLEMENT 

and offered to perform in accordance with the contract. The purchaser must pay agreed cash, less credit for deposit and also deliver a bond and mortgage as agreed upon even though the purchaser sought an improper credit on purchase price.

The court explained the latter holding as "it sometimes happens that parties to a contract of sale disagree as to its meaning. Where good faith attends the closing, the difficulty is talked out, resolved, compromised, or a method for solution agreed upon."2 

§68. Oral contract and part performance. 

Oral agreement for the sale of real property must be certain and the terms of same must be certain and definite to be specifically performed, even though there has been part performance. However, in such case the seller has to pay the value of the purchaser's part performance on the ground that the seller must not become unjustly enriched.¹ 

§72. Other covenants. 

A covenant that any conveyance is subject to boating, fishing and bathing rights in and on a certain lake is a covenant that must be respected by the search of the records and further upon inquiry such covenants should be honored and kept.¹ $74. Time of Performance.

Time of the essence may be extended by written agreement between the parties or their attorneys.¹ 

875. Specific Performance in General.

Specific performance will not be granted where property was taken in the name of a husband and wife which was later revealed that there was not a legal marriage, and during pendency of matrimonial action parties contracted that 

2 Four-G Corp. vs. Ruta 25 N.J. 503 138 A. 2nd 18. 

$59. $68. 

1 Heim vs. Shore 56 N.J.Super. 62, 151 A. 2nd, 556.

1 Camp Clearwater Inc. vs. Plock 59 N.J.Super. 1 157 A. 2, 15.

property be sold at a price to be fixed by arbitrators.' Specific performance will be granted where a purchaser exercised an option to purchase within time, and time for closing was not of the essence or made so by the action of the parties, and suit was instituted immediately and general offer of purchaser to perform in accordance with the contract, the purchaser having acted in good faith." 

Specific performance is a discretionary remedy. Specific performance of a contract to convey realty at the instance of a vendee will not be granted where the vendor shows a complete failure of title. This rule comes within the general doctrine that a court of equity will not compel a party to a contract to perform that which is impossible. Similarly, although a vendee may compel specific performance of a contract where the vendor's title is subject to some encumbrance, his only remedy is by way of a decree ordering the vendor to convey whatever interest he has, and abating the purchase price accordingly. But it is clear that an equity court will not compel a conveyance by a warranty deed where it is aware of some defect in the vendor's title. It is a well recognized maxim that equity will afford complete relief. To compel a conveyance by a warranty deed where there was a reasonable doubt concerning the vendor's title would simply have resulted in a further suit at law on the covenant. However, if a decree for specific performance was granted, that would show that the vendor's title is superior, because specific performance would not be decreed if there was a defect in vendor's title, and therefore the successors in interest of party to such action were barred by res judicata and collateral estoppel from denying validity of vendor's title.³ 

There may be specific sanctions against a purchaser of realty with deferred payments who failed to make payments and a deed is withheld until the final payment. Specific performance will be granted where the contract involves the building $75. 1 Polombo vs. Polombo 48 N.J.Super. 13, 136 A. 2nd, 674.

and sale of a house with an abatement of the purchase price for incomplete or improper workmanship in the building, by the builder." 

§77. Title. 

Usually there shall be no specific performance where the vendor's title is defective unless an abatement in price is asked for and the taking of whatever title the vendor has is consented to by the purchaser. But where specific performance is granted then that denotes a superiority of vendor's title and the successors in interest of party to such action are barred by res judicata and collateral estoppel from denying validity of vendor's title."

A defective contract description taken from the owners deed and which also appeared in deeds of their predecessors in title showing a variance of 25 acres is an unmarketable title and the purchaser cannot be compelled to take title from the seller in an action for specific performance.2 

§81. Effect of Recission. 

To sue on the ground of rescission, it is essential that the misrepresentation alleged to have been practiced by the one party upon the other shall have been of material significance, effective in deceiving the latter and in inducing him to enter into the agreement to his injury. If the evidence reveals that there has been no misrepresentation or that the representation appears to be beyond meritorious criticism then there shall be no rescission.¹ If the contract states that it shall be sold free and clear of all encumbrances, and an easement for a sewer shows up in the title, then the purchasers are entitled to equitable relief of recission.2 

§83. Specific Performance with Abatement in General. 

Although a vendee may compel specific performance of a contract where the vendor's title is subject to some encum- 

5 Koppel vs. Olaf Realty Corp. 56 N.J.Super. 109, 151 A. 2nd 577. $77. 1 Robinson-Shore Development Co. vs. Gallagher 26 N.J. 59, 138 A 2nd, 726. 

2 Taub vs. Nassau Homes Inc. 53 N.J.Super. 209, 147 A. 2nd, 73. 

1 Sheehy vs. Galipeau 48 N.J.Super 95 137 A. 2nd, 5. 

2 Bertrand vs. Jones 58 N.J.Super. 273, 156 A. 2nd, 161. §81. 11

brance, his only remedy is by way of a decree ordering the vendor to convey whatever interest he has, and abating the purchase price accordingly. Even where the vendee does not seek an abatement of the price, but at the trial a dispute develops concerning the sufficience of the vendor's title, and the court determines the vendor's title is defective in some particulars, the appropriate remedy is to compel a conveyance of whatever the vendor has with a proportionate abatement of the purchase price.¹ 

§84. Vendor's Right to and Specific Performance with Abatement in General.

Specific performance will not be granted where there is a defect in the title of the vendor and when it is impossible to perform the contract to convey free from encumbrances. 

The Supreme Court overruled the Appellate Division decreeing conveyance of whatever interest in the property that the vendor has, with or without an abatement in the purchase price.¹ $89. Inability of Vendor to convey.

Where there was an agreement to convey free from encumbrances and a sewer easement is shown up in the title, then the vendor cannot convey free from encumbrances and the purchaser is entitled to rescind the contract and recover deposit money, and attendant legal expenses. Though the contract said it was subject to restrictions, an easement is not a restriction and the vendor was unable to convey free from encumbrances.¹ 

891. Fraud and Misrepresentation.

Misrepresentation may be a ground or rescission but the misrepresentation must be of material significance, effective in deceiving the other party and in inducing him to enter the agreement to his injury.¹

$92. Recission in General.

Where the contract stated that it was subject to restrictions but the title shows a sewer easement and the vendor contracts to convey free from any encumbrances, then the purchaser has the right to rescind the contract. When a purchaser changes her mind about the purchase of the house after the contracts were signed that is no reason for rescission of the contract and subsequent allegations of false and fraudulent representations as to the ownership and cost of a road proved to be untimely and inconsequential to allow the purchaser to rescind the contract on the ground of misrepresentation. It must have been material and induced the purchaser to buy the house on account of the road and when testimony was to the effect that the purchaser was not interested in the road it became immaterial.2 

897. Liquidated Damages. 

Where the purchase price was $34,500.00, with a deposit of $500.00 having been paid and the purchaser did not want to go through with the contract, the vendor selling it for $33,000.00, with the approval and consent of the purchaser to another party, and there was no mala fides on the vendor in the sale of the property, a judgment for $1,000.00 as liquidated damages was held not to be excessive.¹ 898. 

Rights of Vendor on Default of Vendee.

If the vendee refuses to go through with the contract for the sale of real property, then the vendor may keep the deposit and sue for damages after the resale of the property to another party after obtaining the approval of the sale of the property by the purchaser and there is no mala fides on the vendor's part in the resale of the property. The measure of damages is the difference between the contract price and the market value thereof at the time of the breach, less credit for any deposits made on account of purchase price. The re- $92. 1 Bertrand vs. Jones 58 N.J.Super 273, 156 A. 2nd, 161. 

2 Sheehy vs. Galipeau 48 N.J.Super. 95, 137 A. 2nd 5. §97. 1 Sheehy vs. Gallipeau 48 N.J.Super. 95, 137 A. 2nd 5. 13

sale price if made within a reasonable time and under the same circumstances is prima facie, though not conclusive evidence of the market value of the property is admeasuement of damages.¹ Where a purchaser in a development was held to the provisions of the contract, and they allegedly and maliciously threatened to go through with the contract and to immediately sell the home to an undesirable person which would have an adverse effect upon the desirability and success of the development and the builder becomes apprehensive and fearful of his investment, the builder has the right to consider the contract breached and treat it as such, and obtain any damages he suffered from the breach.2 $99. Damages.

The measure of damages to the vendor for the vendee's breach of his executory contract to purchase real estate is the difference between the contract price and the market value thereof at the time of the breach, less a credit for any deposits made on account of the purchase price.¹ If the sewer worked allright for a year and backages of water were caused by obstructions, purchasers were not entitled to recover from vendor for cost of replacing sewer line on the ground that it had been defectively constructed.2 If the vendor cannot convey then the purchaser is entitled to the return of his deposit plus attendant legal expenses, not to counsel fees. If the vendor cannot convey then they are not entitled to damages against the purchaser despite the fact that the purchasers originally asserted an inadequate ground for recission of contract sale.³ $104. 

Definition of Broker. 

A New York broker could obtain a license in New Jersey under the reciprocal privileges provision of N.J.S.A. 

45:15-20. If he fails to do so the court of New Jersey would $98. 1 Sheehy vs. Gallipeau 48 N.J.Super 95, 137 A. 2nd, 95. $99. 

2 Wolf vs. Walton Corp. 57 N.J.Super. 278, 154 A. 2nd, 625. 

1 Sheehy vs. Galipeau 48 N.J. Super. p.102 137 Á. 2nd 5. 

2 Levy vs. C. Young Const. Co. 26 N.J. 330, 138 A. 2nd, 738. 

3 Bertrand vs. Jones 58 N.J.Super 273, 156 A. 2nd, 161. 14

POCKET PART-SUPPLEMENT 

be closed to any action for the collection of his brokerage commission. An unlicensed person may not act as a real estate broker or salesman in this State unless he happens to belong to the class of persons specifically except in R.S. 

45:15-4 attorneys, receivers, trustees in bankruptcy, executors, administrators or persons selling real estate under court order or deed of trust, banks, etc. To do so is to invite a penalty, N.J.S.A. 45:15-23. If an unlicensed person cannot collect a commission on the ground of contract, he cannot collect a commission on the ground of tort for unjustifiable interference with a broker's right to pursue his lawful business and to reap earnings of his industry. In the Supreme 

Court decision of this matter the court held that there was a genuine issue of fact as to the separate work of the New Jersey Broker in the buyer's behalf and that a summary judgment should not be made against him, stating, "the licensing be closed to any action for the collection of his brokerage act is a regulatory measure and represents the strong public policy of our State. Any 'single act' in the execution of the authority to serve as broker in connection with the stimulation or consummation of a sale constitutes engaging in business and requires the actor to have a license. For example, arranging for a prospective buyer to inspect the property brings the mandate into operation. Moreover, a person violating the statute is subject to a monetary penalty and to commitment on failure to pay it.² 

§105. 

Statutory regulation.

The licensing act (Broker's license) is a regulatory measure and represents the strong public policy of the State of 

New Jersey. Any "single act" in the execution of the authority to serve as broker in connection with the stimulation or consummation of a sale constitutes engaging in business and requires the actor to have a license. A New York broker may become a New Jersey Broker under the reciprocity rule. 

Failing to do that and doing business as a real estate broker in New Jersey would subject him to a monetary penalty and

'the right to pursue a lawful business is a property right that the law protects against unjustifiable interference. Any act or omission which unjustifiably disturbs or impedes the enjoyment of such right constitutes its wrongful invasion, and is properly treated as tortious.' Every business entrepreneur has a right to enjoy the fruits and advantages of his own industry, skill and credit, free from unjustified interference, but not as against fair and legitimate competition. 

The intentional interference with the opportunity of a broker to earn a commission is actionable in tort unless the defendants are acting in the exercise of an equal or superior right."1 

Where there is a contract between the broker and the owner that if either sold the property a commission was due to the broker and if the owner made a tentative agreement to sell with others during the 3 month term of the contract, and then induced the broker, in consideration of the sum of $500. to rescind the brokerage contract, and the purchasers acquired the property paying $160,000.00 for it, the court reinstated the brokerage contract, which he had found to have been fraudulently rescinded and awarded the broker damages in the sum of $15,000. 10% of $160,000 less the $500. Where there was no evidence of a giving of notice of intention to file in writing to the owner by mechanics' lien claimant either personally or by registered mail then summary judgment may be given to the homeowner.³ "The purpose of the Mechanics Lien Act is very aptly expressed in Friedman vs. Stein, 4 N.J. 34, 40-41 (1950); "The mechanic's and materialman's liens had their genesis in the civil law. They are unknown to the common law; and they had no recognition in equity except as prescribed by statute. Thus it is that these liens are exclusively statutory in origin, and being in derogation of the common law, the provisions of the statute giving rise to the lien are to be strictly construed, while the provisions for the enforcement of the lien thereby created are to be liberally construed to effectuate the remedial statutory policy of providing priority of payment of the price or value of work performed and materials furnished in the erection or reparation of a building or other structure and thus wise the security of the land and buildings for the payment of that which has made for an assumed enhancement of the value of the property.'

The claim by a supplier for materials furnished is an inchoate lien until all statutory requisites are met, at which time the statutory lien arises." 4

It has been pointed out that mechanics' and materialmen's liens in New Jersey are exclusively statutory in origin, and being in derogation of the common law, the provisions of the statute giving rise to the lien are to be strictly construed, while those relating to the enforcement of the lien created are to be liberally construed to effectuate the remedial statutory policy of rendering the lands and buildings collaterally liable for the payment of the price or value of work performed and materials furnished in the erection or repair of the buildings. The statutory lien, being a cumulative remedy for the enforcement of the underlying debt, does not materialize until all the statutory requisites are met. A claimant's status is that of a general creditor only until he has done all things necessary to give him the security of the statutory lien. §313. 2 Easements. A common owner acquired by reversion a piece of land over which a railroad right of way was made but never fulfilled and later abandoned and conveyed certain lots to another person did not create an implied easement, or an easement by implied reservation, or a quasi-easement, or an easement of necessity to have access to the ocean and that the proofs were therefore insufficient. Where a party conveys all his right, title and interest in an easement he no longer becomes an indispensable party in an action involving the easement. An "easement" is an encumbrance, or a grant of a legal estate, distinct from ownership, to use in some way the land of another. A reservation in a deed of an easement is the creation in behalf of the grantor of a new right issuing out of the thing granted. "Whether a deed created an easement is to be determined by ascertaining the intention of the parties as gathered from the language of the instrument. **** No particular words are necessary to constitute a grant, and any words which clearly show the intention to give an easement**** are sufficient to effect that purpose, provided the language is certain and definite in its terms.****

It is true that in this State the provisions of the Recording 

Act (R.S. 46:21-1) are deemed not to charge a purchaser of lands with constructive notice of easements or covenants affecting his lands which appear in an earlier deed by his grantor, conveying property other than the property involved in the purchaser's chain of title. Having said that, however, is not to dispose of the question, for notice of the existence of an easement affecting his property may be chargeable to a purchaser by means other than the recording statute-for example, by notice from a physical inspection of the property or from recital in a deed in his chain of title. A purchaser is "chargeable" with notice of every mat- $313. 1 Adams vs. Cole 48 N.J.Super 119, 137 A. 2nd, 92.

Plaintiff is charged with such notice by the recital contained in every deed in its chain of title."5 $317.

Easements created by implication. "An easement by implied reservation is more difficult to prove and establish that that of an implied grant. An easement by way of implied reservation requires consideration of the theory of quasi-easement. It arises as the result of the apparent use by the common owner at the time of the conveyance of a portion of the property usually denominated "quasiservient tenement." The "quasi-dominant tenement," the remaining portion of the premises in the hands of the grantor, may have the benefit of this easement if the grantor can establish certain well-recognized requirements, as of the time of conveyance. This theory is an exception to the general rule that a grantor may not be heard in derogation of his grant." "The basic principle upon which rests the creation of easements by implied grant was stated in A. J. & J. O.

Easement of necessity. "Although the adjective "necessary" is used in setting forth one of the factors indicative of the existence of a quasieasement, it is to be noted that this element of evidence of intent differs from the "necessity" required in establishing a 'way of necessity'. What is intended here is nothing greater than a reasonable necessity, and not the absolute necessity required in establishing an 'easement of necessity." An easement that provides that the landowner is to have and maintain drainage pipes as they now exist charges the owner with at least constructive knowledge of this easement in its chain of title. The court directs the owner to provide the other property owner with an adequate drainage system on his property. If it could be done by filter bed, or lateral trenches, or by connecting plaintiff's drainage pipe to the sewage disposal system constructed by the landowner on its property at the time the new building was erected, it had to be done as ordered by the court and it cast no undue burden on the landowner. "Easements of necessity arise by virtue of inference or implication of an intent implied from the facts surrounding the primary conveyance. They are not the result of a direct express grant. *** 'necessity' however, which may give rise to an easement does not necessarily connote that without an easement the dominant tenant could have no beneficial enjoyment of his lands whatsoever, and the word should be understood as meaning 'reasonably necessary for convenient, comfortable or beneficial enjoyment.' If the record indicates that the tract was used as a onefamily estate for nearly 30 years then the touchstone is necessity and not convenience and a beach cannot be constructed. The primary rule of construction is that the intent of the conveyor is normally determined by the language of the conveyance read as an entirety and in the light of the surrounding circumstances. The language being unambiguous then the terms of the instrument govern. In determining the circumstances the aesthetic considerations weigh heavily in the balance. Modern urban and suburban property owners are protected in their modes of living by zoning laws, whereas in the county the more practical and convenient devices for securing the self-same lawful ends are restrictive covenants. The law will protect and give effect to provisions designed to achieve these ends. The court held, "it is our conclusion that the provisions in the deed as a whole are in restrictive tones. They do not have that fluidity and flexibility essential to the allowance of a transgression because of convenience or even reasonable necessity, but rather that fixity ordinarily requiring a punctilio of performance. Nor do the circumstances surrounding the formulation of the conveyance necessitate a contra result. Many of the background factors have previously been alluded to and they point to the conclusion that a strict adherence to the language was intended. To these might be added the fact that in the absence of evidence establishing the contrary, it must be assumed that the parties were familiar with the property and that they had knowledge of the depth of the pond within the area." 99 1

§391. Deeds in General. 

"We recognize, as the cited cases indicate, that the prime consideration in determining the extent of a grant is the intention of the parties. The burden of proof in showing such intention is upon the party claiming title **** In ascertain ing the intention of the parties the ancient deeds must be construed in the light of the circumstances existing at the time of the grants." Where the city provided that the grantee was to fill and grade according to specifications and was to do it within one year and time was not made of the essence and the city extended the time the condition subsequent was waived and it was not a limitation or fee simple determinable. "While language is primary guide for ascertainment of whether a deed attempts to condition or limit an estate, it is the instrument as a whole, and not a particular phrase which provides the basis for the attainment of the effectuation of the intent of the parties. *** If the four corners of deed provide a coherent expression of the parties' intent, the court need search no further, but if an ambiguity or reasonable doubt appears from a perusal of the particular symbols of expression, the court's horizons must be broadened to encompass the circumstances surrounding the transaction. ***

Eminent Domain: 

The Supreme Court in upholding the constitutionality of the Commissioner of Conservation and Economic Develop ment to condemn land for the so-called "Round Valley" water supply, held: ""Eminent domain' is the power of the State to take private property for public use, for which 'just compensation' must be made in virtue of the 1947 State Constitution Art. I, par. 20, and the Fourteenth 

Amendment to the Federal Constitution. It is a right founded in the law of necessity which is inherent in sovereignty and essential to the existence of all government, even in its most primitive forms, a right exercised by the Romans, whence came the term 'eminent domain,' in the construction of road, acqueducts and similar public works. The constitutional provision is not the source, but rather a limitation upon the exercise of this attribute of sovereignty; and it would seem to be basic to this conception that 'when the use to which the property taken is applied is public, the propriety or expediency of the appropriation cannot be called in question by any other authority. The exercise of the State's sovereign power is the exclusive province of the legislative branch of the government; and since the Legislature cannot in all cases directly supervise the taking of property by eminent domain, it may delegate the exercise of the right. It is for the Legislature to determine what constitutes a public use, a finding beyond judicial interference unless it be unreasonable and arbitrary and a perversion of the power."

Where a contract was made to pay $5,000.00 per acre for an assemblage of 400 acres provided the vendor could deliver title in 60 days and also a provision that he would not be penalized if he could not deliver title because of alignment of proposed turnpike extension the Supreme Court held that the contract was not admissible to show value of portion of vendor's land taken and damaged by condemnation, stating: "An owner of lands sought to be condemned is entitled to their 'market value fairly determined." That value may reflect not only the use to which the property is presently devoted but also that use to which it may be readily converted. In that connection the value may be determined in light of the special or higher use of the land when combined with other parcels; it need not be measured merely by the use to which the land is or can be put as a separate tract. 

But in order for that special adaptability to be considered, there must be a reasonable probability of the lands in question being combined with other tracts for that purpose in the reasonably near future. In absence of such a showing, the chance of their being combined with other tracts for that purpose in the reasonably near future. Inabsence of such a showing, the chance of their being united for that special use is regarded 'as too remote and speculative to have any legitimate effect upon the valuation." 2

The Blighted Area Act was held constitutional by the Supreme Court and that the hearing required under the act is legislative in character and not judicial.³ 

Where a lot is taken as a corner lot when the complaint was filed, it having be an inside lot prior to that, the Supreme 

Court held: "Where the State Highway Commission condemns land for highway purposes it must pay compensation in accordance with constitutional and statutory requirements. The proper measure of compensation is generally said to be the fair market value at the time of the institution of his condemnation proceeding. Where the taking coincides with the institution of the condemnation proceeding, this measure of compensation operates justly insofar as both the condemnor and condemnee are concerned. However, where there is a taking followed by a change in the condition of the property before the institution of the condemnation proceeding, different con siderations apply. Under R.S. 27:7-22 the State Highway Com mission is authorized to enter upon and take property in advance of the institution of his condemnation action, although the statute would seem to contemplace no significant delay between the taking and the legal proceeding. If, after the taking and before the condemnation action, the property is reduced in value because of alterations by the Commissioner or otherwise, the condemnee would justly be entitled to the high valuation as of the date of taking. 

2 N.J. Turnpike vs. Bowley 27 N.J. 549, 143 A. 2nd, 558. 

3 Wilson vs. Long Branch 27 N.J. 360, 142 A. 2nd, 837. 31

**** On the other hand if, after the taking and before the condemnation action, the land increases in value because of alterations by the Commissioner or otherwise, the condemnee would not justly be entitled to the higher valuation." 4 Condemnation

Where the Law Division entered a judgment upon a jury award of $8,200. as compensation for the taking of a part of the owner's property under a condemnation award the Supreme Court held it was reversible error for the trial judge to state no functional relationship existed between condemned and non-condemned portions of lot which substantially destroyed effect of the owner's expert testimony by instructing jurors that they could find it worthless if there was no functional relationship between the condemned and non-condemned portions of lot and further holding that "where only a part of an owner's land is taken he is entitled to be compensated not only for the value of the land taken but also for any diminution in the value of his remaining land which may be attributed to the taking. However, it is entirely conceivable that under certain circumstances severance will not effect a change in the value of the remaining portion of an owner's property."

The procedure was succinctly stated in State vs. Fisher in condemnation cases. It was as follows: "From the procedural standpoint (and here we refer to procedure in the sense of judicial power) we are of the opinion that the Law Division has no power in any way to deal with or alter the award of the Commissioners set forth in their filed report, absent any appeal (R.S. 20:1-16 as amended; R.R. 4:92-6 and 7), In New Jersey, the soverign power of eminent domain resides in the legislative branch of the government. The manner and scope of its exercise must necessarily be as prescribed by legislation. Courts only come into the process in the way and to the extent that the Legislature directs and properly defer to the legislative will as to the mode by which compensation shall be determined. Judicial participation is limited by our present statute to determination of 'an action in the Superior Court for the appointment of three commissioners to fix the compensation to be paid' (R.S. 20:1-2, as amended) and to hearing an appeal from the report by any dissatisfied party, with or without a jury (R.S. 20:1-16 as amended), which amounts to a complete trial de novo without regard or reference to the award of the Commissioners. The initial action is a very limited one and is concluded by a final judgment of appointment. After its entry no further action is required or possible by 

4 State vs. Jones 27 N.J. 257, 142 A. 2nd, 232. 

Ridgewood vs. Sreel Investment 28 N.J. 121, 145 A. 2nd 306. 32

POCKET PART-SUPPLEMENT 

the trial court (except extension of time to file the report, the appointment of substituted Commissioners and the allowance of their fees and expenses (R.R. 4:92-4 and 5) and the hearing of a claim by the owner in the action to recover the amount awarded (R.S. 20:1-13 as amended). The report is simply filed and need not not be confirmed. The appeal is in practical effect an entirely new action resulting in another final judgment. It follows from these fundamentals that the Law Division has no power to correct or alter the filed report or even to order the resubmission of the subject matter or any part to the Commissioners.” 6

Where the judge instructed the jury that their deliberations were to be governed by the testimony it was upheld by the Appellate Division despite the fact that the court also instructed the jury that their view of the land seemed to be controlling. Evidence of sale 1/2 mile away was correctly stricken, because the judge's discretion in this regard is broad. The measure of damages for taking of land in condemnation cases is the value of land as of the date of taking, determined by what would be agreeable to a buyer and seller acting without compulsion. 

Where the taking of property by condemnation in effect landlocked the owner because he had a pre-existing easement of access to and from a public highway it was a taking of his property without just compensation even though he does not own the fee, the roadway being subject to the easement. "A pre-existing easement of access to and from a public highway, possessed by an owner of land abutting on such highway, constitutes a right of property of which he cannot be deprived without just compensation, and it is of no consequence that he does not own in fee the roadway subject to the easement." 8 "Just compensation means the payment of such a sum of money to the owner of the property taken or damages as will make him whole; that is, upon the receipt by him of the compensation and damage awarded he will not be any poorer by reason of his property being so taken or damaged." • State vs. Fisher 54 N.J.Super. 274, 148 A. 2nd, 135.

Just 

7 State vs. Gorga by State Highway Comm. vs. Gorga 54 N.J.Super. 

520, 149 A. 2nd, 266. 

8 Mueller vs. N.J. Highway Authority 59 N.J.Super. 583, 158 A. 2nd, 343. 349. 

Port of New York Authority vs. Howell 56 N.J.Super. 343, 153 A. 

33

compensation has been interpreted by the learned Judge of the Superior Court to mean the following: 

1. A figure that will be fair and just, not only to the property owner, but also to the condemning authority. 

2. It shall be real, substantial, full and ample. 

3. The fair price for any permitted use for which the land has a commercial value of its own in the immediate present or in reasonable anticipation in the near future. 

4. The market value, having the reasonably anticipated permitted use in view. 

5. The rule limits the proof to the value of the land as of the control date. i.e. the date of taking, in the condition of the land at time and to the uses to which it is naturally adapted and restricted. 

6. It excludes speculative and possible use if improvements and changes were made. Matters that are collateral, involving calculation of costs and profits, are too remote. 

7. Local planning and zoning regulations in effect as of the control date are material and relevant on the question of usability and have a vital bearing on the commercial value of the land. 

8. Buildings, trees, shrubs, top soil etc. underlying stone, sand and gravel are component parts of the land and are not to be valued separately and apart from the land, but it may be shown to what extent the land is enhanced in value thereby, subject, of course to the use restrictions imposed by valid local zoning regulations. 

9. A test what a willing buyer not compelled to buy would give to a willing seller not compelled to sell. 

10. Another test-to consider the total value of the property before anything is done by the condemning authority, then consider the value after the property has been taken. The difference between the two should represent the amount of money the property owner should receive. 

11. In the absence of a statute authorizing it, injuries to a business or a loss of profits resulting from the condemnation of a fee is not an element of damage in determining just compensation for the permanent taking of that fee. 

12. It does not include future loss of profits, expenses of moving removable fixtures and personal property from the premises, the loss of good will which inheres in the location, or other like consequential losses which would ensue from the sale of the property to someone other than the soverign or condemning authority. 

13. Income derived from a business conducted depends upon various factors not attributable to the land and furnishes no criterion for the determination of the market value thereof. 

14. The testimony of real estate experts is entitled to respect and be weighed by the trier of the facts as part of the evidence and and is to aid and assist the trier of the facts, not dominate or control him in the decision of the disputed question. 

15. A view by the court will help in forming its judgment. 34

POCKET PART-SUPPLEMENT 

Interest should be allowed as of the date on which possession is taken of condemned land on equitable principles or on the basis of specific statutory provision, possession meaning the actual control by physical occupation and the holding and exercise of dominion over it and the use and control of land to the exclusion of all others from like use or control.10 

The rule that the appeal should be taken within ten days after service of a copy of a report by the Condemnation commissioners should not be harshly construed to bar remedy, and certainly not as against State.¹¹
